The Signature Is the Real Decision: What Every TRON User Should Know Before Approving a Crypto Swap

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Swap TRC-20 tokens on TRON with self-custody and open-source smart contracts. Low fees, verifiable on TRONSCAN.

A crypto transaction can look deceptively simple.

Connect a wallet. Select a token. Enter an amount. Press Swap.

Then your wallet opens a confirmation window.

Most users glance at the screen, check whether the numbers look right, and approve.

But this is arguably the most important moment of the entire interaction.

Because connecting a wallet, approving token access, and executing a swap are not necessarily the same operation. Understanding the difference is one of the most useful habits a Web3 user can develop.

One of the most common misconceptions in DeFi is that connecting a wallet automatically gives an application access to its funds.

A wallet connection generally allows an application to interact with a wallet address and request certain actions. The user still needs to authorize transactions that require a signature.

There are three separate concepts worth understanding:

Wallet connection: An application establishes communication with your wallet.

Token approval: You authorize a particular smart contract to spend a specified amount of a token on your behalf.

Swap execution: A transaction interacts with the relevant smart contract to perform the exchange.

Depending on the protocol and token, the exact transaction sequence can differ.

Understanding which operation you are signing is more important than simply recognizing the application's name.

Let's take the TRC-20 standard on TRON as an example.

TRC-20 tokens implement a permission mechanism that includes two important functions:

The first authorizes a designated address to spend up to a specified amount of tokens.

The second lets an application or user check how much spending permission remains.

Before a smart contract can transfer those tokens using the standard delegated-transfer mechanism, it may need an allowance from your wallet.

This is where token approval enters the picture.

However, there is an important distinction between approving 250 USDT and granting an unlimited allowance.

The latter can authorize substantially more spending than the immediate transaction requires.

For users, the practical lesson is straightforward:

Don't treat every approval request as a routine confirmation.

Check the spender address and the requested amount. Understand what you are authorizing.

The official TRON documentation provides detailed explanations of the TRC-20 interface and contract interaction:

https://developers.tron.network/docs/trc20-protocol-interface

They can reduce repeated authorization steps when interacting with the same application.

But convenience and security are not interchangeable.

If a contract has permission to spend a token balance, that permission may remain active beyond the transaction that originally required it.

This creates an important security consideration.

A user who approves a contract once might forget that the authorization still exists weeks or months later.

A better approach is to periodically review token allowances and revoke permissions that are no longer needed.

This is particularly relevant for people who interact with multiple DeFi applications.

Your wallet balance is only one part of your security picture. Existing permissions deserve attention too.

There is another detail that makes TRON interesting from an engineering perspective.

Unlike networks that primarily describe transaction execution costs through a single gas metric, TRON uses two principal resources:

Bandwidth measures the data size of an on-chain transaction.

Energy measures the computational resources required to execute smart contracts.

A TRC-20 interaction generally consumes both.

The amount of Energy depends on the contract execution itself. Different operations can have different resource requirements.

For users, this means transaction costs are not simply a fixed number attached to every swap.

They depend on the operation, available resources, and applicable network parameters.

TRON also supports Energy delegation and mechanisms that allow smart contract developers to cover part of the Energy cost.

This creates opportunities for applications to improve the transaction experience without requiring every user to manage network resources in the same way.

https://developers.tron.network/docs/bandwidth-and-energy

Whether you are exchanging stablecoins, moving TRX, or interacting with a new DeFi protocol, take a moment to inspect the transaction.

Make sure the application and wallet are interacting with the intended blockchain.

Check the token contract address, not just its ticker or display name.

Is the wallet asking for a token approval or an actual transaction?

Make sure the address requesting token permission corresponds to the contract you intended to use.

Avoid granting broader permissions than you are comfortable with.

Understand whether the operation requires Energy, Bandwidth, or additional TRX.

After execution, inspect the transaction on a blockchain explorer rather than relying exclusively on an application's success notification.

These steps take very little time but can help prevent expensive mistakes.

These principles become particularly relevant when evaluating applications built specifically for the TRON ecosystem.

TronSeven is a non-custodial swap platform focused on TRON assets, including TRX and TRC-20 tokens such as USDT and USDC.

The platform supports wallet connectivity through TronLink, Trust Wallet, and WalletConnect.

There are several aspects worth exploring:

TRON-native experience: The platform focuses on token exchanges within the TRON ecosystem.

Non-custodial architecture: Its published model allows users to interact through their wallets without the platform taking custody of their assets.

Public smart contract information: The project publishes its contract address and provides access to TRONSCAN.

Open-source approach: Its website provides access to smart contract source information and references security reviews by CertiK and SlowMist.

Network resource awareness: The platform describes using TRON Energy to cover network costs associated with swaps.

These are project-published characteristics, and anyone evaluating the platform should independently inspect the relevant contract, documentation, and transaction history.

You can explore the application and its technical information here:

Crypto applications have become increasingly polished.

Interfaces are faster. Wallet integrations are more accessible. Transactions that once required considerable technical knowledge can now be initiated with a few clicks.

But there is still a gap between making an operation easy to perform and making it easy to understand.

A good DeFi experience should help users understand what they are authorizing, which contract they are interacting with, what resources an operation requires, and how to verify the result.

This is not just a security issue. It is a product-design issue.

The next generation of Web3 applications should not compete exclusively on the number of clicks required to complete a transaction.

They should compete on how much confidence users can have in every decision along the way.

Because in decentralized finance, the most important button isn't necessarily the one that says Swap.

Transactional SMS Alerts Service: Signup Suppression and Delivery Tracking

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The smallest sending integration can still leave substantial application work around delivery tracking. TL;DR: for a property-management signup flow, choose the transactional SMS service whose delivery evidence your team can reconcile, then measure tenant verification separately. A direct HTTP API reduces client integration work. Background reconciliation is the corresponding responsibility when updates arrive through polling.

Start with evidence. A successful send request doesn't prove that a prospective tenant received the verification link, clicked it, or finished creating an account. Those are different observations. Keeping them separate makes this service comparison useful after the first successful request.

Twilio belongs on the shortlist when SMS is the required channel. Pick it when its documented messaging workflow fits the sender configuration and operational practices your team is prepared to maintain. During evaluation, trace one verification attempt from application request through delivery evidence. Ask who owns each transition. Familiarity with a vendor name cannot answer that question, and this comparison establishes no delivery-rate advantage.

Resend is relevant when email verification is an acceptable product choice or a separately designed fallback. Pick that route when the application can own the verification token and email flow. An email integration changes the channel decision, so compare it against the actual signup requirement before treating it as an SMS alternative. If a tenant explicitly needs a text message, an email-only path leaves that requirement unresolved.

Amazon SES provides another email option to evaluate. Pick it when its documented sending workflow fits the infrastructure your team intends to operate. Compare its setup and operational responsibilities with Resend's rather than counting both as equivalent SMS transports. Neither email choice resolves a mandatory SMS requirement. Their relevance here is the product decision to permit verification by email, not an assumed equivalence between channels.

Infrai offers a plain REST API with no SDK to install, plus a single API key and unified billing across 295 routes in 20 modules. Anything that can send an HTTP request can call it, without a client library version to maintain. For a property portal adding email fallback, that shared credential and consolidated billing remove a second provider key to provision and a separate invoice to reconcile. The application still owns channel selection and verification logic. Public, self-describing discovery is another concrete benefit: request and response schemas can be inspected without a key, so the sender owner and reconciliation-worker owner can agree on the contract before provisioning credentials. Runnable examples in 10 languages help those teams inspect the same interface even when their runtimes differ.

For that direct REST option, delivery reconciliation uses polling rather than webhook events. That is a concrete trade-off: less client-library maintenance, but an application worker must collect delivery updates. Resend support helps recover a missed message without rebuilding the original payload flow. It still needs an application decision about whether the verification link remains valid.

Diagram in words: signup attempt → suppression and abuse checks → send request → stored message identifier → delivery reconciliation → verification-link redemption.

There are two branches after sending. One records transport evidence. The other records whether the tenant completed verification.

Connect both to an application-generated attempt identifier, but don't collapse them into one success flag. In a property portal, a delivered message with an unused link asks a different operational question from a request that never reached the provider. The first deserves a look at the signup experience; the second needs investigation of the sending path. Consider a synthetic signup where a receipt arrives but the tenant leaves the page before redeeming the link. The transport dashboard can correctly show delivery while the account remains unverified. If the reminder job reads only that transport flag, it may suppress a reminder that the product intended to offer; if it reads only the unverified account flag, it may send another text without checking a recent tenant request. The useful record combines the attempt, the delivery observation, token validity, and the next permitted action. No single provider status can carry all that application meaning.

Record the attempt identifier, reconciliation timestamps, and application state needed to explain the next action. Keep phone numbers and verification links out of the example log stream. A verification link is an account credential, not a debugging label.

Use three separate measurements: send requests accepted, delivery observations collected, and signup attempts verified. Also measure the age of the last reconciliation. A quiet delivery dashboard can mean there's nothing new to report; it can also mean the worker has stopped collecting evidence. Without freshness, those situations look identical. Set the alert threshold from the signup experience the product actually promises, rather than adopting an arbitrary universal interval.

This TypeScript example checks one existing SMS message. Set SMS_STATUS_URL to its documented status URL, constructed from the discovery path and your stored message identifier. Set the API-key environment variable separately. Use trusted server configuration for the URL, restricted to the provider's documented API origin; never accept it from tenant input. No credentials belong in the URL.

Run the file with your existing TypeScript runner in a server runtime with global fetch. The example records application-owned observation fields and leaves the response schema for an adapter to interpret. It performs a read, so retries cannot create another message.

The limit of 4 attempts is an illustrative retry budget, not a service guarantee. The fallback retry delays are 1, 2, and 4 seconds; a longer Retry-After takes precedence. A successful read gives the adapter fresh evidence; it doesn't mark the tenant verified.

Persist a reconciliation timestamp only after the application has validated and stored the observation. Don't copy the example's static attempt identifier into production.

The adapter has a narrow job: map documented responses into application states and retain the provider message identifier. Unknown statuses should remain unresolved until the mapping handles them. Guessing that an unfamiliar value means delivered corrupts the signup funnel. Keep request failures in the worker's operational logs with appropriate access controls; don't show raw provider errors to a tenant.

This is the useful before-and-after: before reconciliation, the dashboard knows that a send was requested; afterward, it has a timestamped delivery observation. Only link redemption advances account verification. Those distinctions make a stalled signup diagnosable without claiming that transport receipts explain every user action.

An initial signup link usually needs prompt dispatch. If a reminder is scheduled, record its intended send time and link expiration together. Recheck suppression, verification completion, and token validity when the job executes. A tenant may finish signing up while the reminder waits. Sending it anyway creates duplicate work for the tenant and misleading volume in the dashboard.

Treat a resend as an explicit application decision. Persist its relationship to the original attempt, and recheck suppression before dispatch. For writes, use the documented idempotency mechanism so retrying an uncertain request cannot silently become a second intended send. Keep one key for one logical operation; don't reuse it for a newly authorized reminder. The read-only example doesn't implement this write path.

Suppression needs its own evidence. Record whether the application allowed a send and why, using internal reason codes rather than logging a tenant's phone number. Count suppressed jobs separately from provider rejections. Otherwise a deliberate decision to honor suppression looks like a sending failure, which sends an operator toward the wrong fix.

Polling is a good fit only if the team can own background reconciliation and define an acceptable freshness budget. Choose a different workflow when immediate webhook-driven orchestration is a requirement. The direct REST option covers SMS and email; a voice, WhatsApp, RCS, or SMTP-relay requirement changes the shortlist. Email fallback also requires application-owned verification tokens. Don't assume a managed email OTP flow.

Keep country restrictions and country-sensitive spending thresholds in the application. Suppression alone doesn't prevent signup abuse. This note establishes no US/EU delivery-rate, latency, or compliance guarantee; evaluate sender requirements and delivery behavior for the countries the property portal actually serves.

References for evaluating the alternatives and the verification experience:

I Spent 10x Longer Debugging AI Code Than Writing It — Here's What Changed

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Everyone talks about AI speeding up coding. Nobody talks about the debugging session from hell that follows.

I’m not exaggerating when I say this: last quarter, I spent roughly 40 hours writing code with AI assistance, and about 380 hours debugging it. That’s not a typo. A 10x ratio. I felt like I was using a tool that wrote perfect-looking code with hidden landmines on every line.

Let me walk you through what happened, why it happened, and the exact workflow shift that flipped those numbers around.

It started innocently. I was building a data pipeline for a client who needed real-time sentiment analysis on Twitter streams. The architecture was straightforward: ingest tweets, run them through a sentiment model, store results in a Postgres database, and expose a simple REST API.

I decided to let an AI assistant generate the initial scaffolding. "Write a Python script that connects to the Twitter API v2, filters for specific keywords, and streams tweets to a Kafka topic," I typed.

Within seconds, I had a 200-line script that looked flawless. Proper type hints. Docstrings. Even a retry mechanism with exponential backoff. I was sold. I pasted it in, ran it, and... it worked. Tweets were flowing into Kafka.

The first issue surfaced two days later. The script crashed at 3 AM because the Twitter API returned a 429 Too Many Requests — the rate limit had been hit. The AI's retry logic was there, but it retried immediately after a 429, which made things worse. I hadn't read the code carefully because it looked right. My mistake.

Each of these took hours to find because the code compiled and ran. No exceptions. No obvious errors. Just wrong behavior in edge cases.

I kept a log of my debugging sessions for a month. Here's the breakdown:

The code was correct in the happy path. It was catastrophically wrong in the edge cases. And the more "senior" the AI tried to sound, the more convoluted the code became.

After three weeks of this, I had a conversation with a friend who runs a fintech startup. He said something that stuck:

"You wouldn't let a junior dev push code to production without a code review. Why are you letting an AI do it?"

He was right. I was treating the AI as a senior architect when I should have been treating it as a very fast junior developer. The code quality was junior-level: it worked for the demo, but didn't handle production realities.

So I changed my approach. Here's the workflow that cut my debugging time from 10x to about 1.5x.

Instead of: "Write a complete function to parse this JSON and insert into the DB."

I now ask: "Give me a function signature and a skeleton. I'll fill in the logic."

This forces me to understand the structure before I accept the code. The AI's job is to save me typing time, not to save me thinking time.

This was the game-changer. Before I let it generate any implementation, I'd ask for unit tests that define the expected behavior.

Then I'd look at the tests, approve them, and then ask for the implementation. This caught 70% of the edge-case bugs before they ever hit my codebase. The AI's tests were often wrong too — but it was much easier to correct a test than to debug a runtime error in production.

These were the sources of 90% of my "what the hell is this doing?" moments. The code got longer, sure. But it became readable. And readable code is debuggable code.

Before accepting any AI suggestion, I'd run it through a diff tool against the existing codebase. Not just to see what changed, but to check if the changes were minimal.

The AI has a tendency to refactor unrelated parts of your code "for consistency." That's how you end up with a PR that changes 400 lines to fix a one-line bug.

The biggest single improvement was providing the AI with real data samples and actual error messages from my logs.

Instead of: "Fix the rate limiting issue."

I'd write: "We're getting 429s from the Twitter API. The current code uses time.sleep(1) between retries. Here's the exact response header that comes back with the retry-after value: retry_after: 900. Implement a retry that respects this header and logs a warning."

The quality of output improved exponentially. The AI wasn't guessing anymore — it was solving a specific puzzle.

Here's something nobody told me when I started this journey: the same AI prompt can give you wildly different results depending on the model version, the random seed, or even the time of day.

I had one instance where I asked for a date parser three times in the same afternoon. The first output handled ISO formats. The second handled MM/DD/YYYY. The third was a custom parser that only worked for YYYY-MM-DD but looked like it handled everything.

That inconsistency is a hidden tax on your time. If you're building a serious project, you need a stable API endpoint that doesn't change behavior under your feet.

This is where I landed on using a consistent API gateway for my AI calls. I was experimenting with different providers, and the pricing and reliability were all over the place. One provider would have a 5-second latency spike right when I was on a deadline. Another would silently cap my token usage mid-request.

After trying a bunch of options, I settled on a pay-as-you-go setup that routes through a unified endpoint — I'm using shadie-oneapi.com these days. It's not flashy, but it's predictable. Same model, same response format, no surprise quota resets. That consistency alone saved me from having to re-verify AI outputs every time I changed a config file.

After two months with this workflow, I re-ran my time tracking:

Total time dropped from 420 hours to 137 hours. A 67% reduction in overall time, and a massive drop in the frustration quotient.

The extra 15 hours I spent writing code "manually" (or at least reviewing it carefully) paid for itself tenfold by eliminating the mystery bugs.

AI coding tools are incredible for generating skeletons — the boring stuff you already know how to write. They are terrible at handling edge cases you haven't thought of yet, because they don't know what you know about your domain.

The fix isn't to stop using AI. The fix is to stop treating it as an autonomous engineer and start treating it as a very fast, slightly clueless intern who needs explicit instructions, code reviews, and unit tests.

And if you're going to rely on AI-generated code, make sure your API layer is stable. Nothing kills momentum faster than your code generation tool changing behavior mid-sprint. A consistent, pay-as-you-go API endpoint removed that variable from my equation, and it's one less thing I have to think about while I'm wrestling with the actual bugs.

The code I write now is less "elegant" — but it runs. And honestly? That's all that matters at 2 AM when production is on fire.

Sort your defects before you buy an automated inspection system

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An automated inspection system is any setup of sensors, fixtures and software that judges a part against a spec with no person deciding each case. Search results treat the term as a synonym for machine vision. A leak tester, a coordinate measuring machine (CMM) and a bed of nails on a circuit board are all automated inspection too, and none of them uses a camera.

We wrote the longer version on our blog, Automated Inspection Systems: Three Families, Not One. This post is the part an engineer can run on a real line this week: classify the defects first.

Each family is defined by what it physically measures.

Pull defects from reject records. Memory is a bad source. Then tag each row:

Coverage is its own column because it moves the budget more than the sensor does. A CMM cannot keep up with a fast line, which is why metrology is usually sampled and vision is usually inline.

Two families on one line is normal. A pump housing might get inline vision for assembly, a leak test on every unit and a sampled CMM check on the critical bore.

Watch for defects that sit between families. Surface porosity that only matters because it leaks is a behaviour problem in a visual disguise, and the leak test settles it faster.

Dev Station builds the record layer around these stations: result store, operator review screen, gauge integration over Bluetooth or serial. The full breakdown of families, coverage and cost is in the original article.

A Crypto Swap Is a Distributed Systems Problem Disguised as a Button

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Swap TRC-20 tokens on TRON with self-custody and open-source smart contracts. Low fees, verifiable on TRONSCAN.

Why transaction states, wallet permissions, liquidity and on-chain verification matter more than a beautiful exchange interface

Select an asset. Enter an amount. Choose what you want to receive. Confirm.

But beneath those four actions is a surprisingly complex coordination problem involving wallets, smart contracts, network resources, liquidity and blockchain settlement.

The interface might take milliseconds to respond. The blockchain has its own timing, rules and failure conditions.

And that difference is where good swap engineering begins.

One of the most important distinctions in Web3 application design is the difference between a user interface state and an actual blockchain state.

The application calculates an estimated exchange.

A displayed quote does not mean a transaction has happened. A wallet approval does not necessarily mean a swap has completed. Even a submitted transaction can remain pending before final confirmation.

A robust application should communicate these states separately.

This is particularly important for developers designing interfaces where users expect immediate feedback.

Wallet connections are often treated as a simple onboarding feature.

They should be treated as a security boundary.

There is a fundamental difference between connecting a wallet and authorizing a transaction.

Users should never be encouraged to approve something they do not understand.

Developers should also avoid requesting unnecessary permissions and should explain potentially risky approval scopes.

Blockchain applications have an advantage over many traditional financial applications: their transaction activity can be independently inspected.

But transparency is useful only when users know what to inspect.

For a token swap protocol, several questions matter:

Contract identity: Is the user interacting with the expected contract?

Source verification: Is the published source code available and verified?

Permissions: Can an administrator change important contract parameters or withdraw assets?

Transaction history: Can users inspect actual operations on-chain?

Security review: Are audit reports available, and do they cover the deployed contract version?

An audit badge is not a substitute for understanding the underlying contract.

Likewise, open-source code does not automatically mean that a deployed application is safe.

The relationship between published code, deployed bytecode and actual permissions deserves careful attention.

Developers sometimes focus heavily on exchange rates while overlooking transaction costs.

On TRON, network resources such as Energy and Bandwidth influence how transactions are executed.

This creates an interesting UX challenge.

A user might understand the token exchange perfectly but still be confused by network-resource requirements.

A well-designed swap interface should make transaction costs understandable before confirmation.

The goal is not simply to make transactions cheaper.

It is to make the cost model predictable enough that users can make informed decisions.

A mature swap application should be designed around failure scenarios, not just the happy path.

The wallet disconnects during the process?

These scenarios should have clear interface responses.

A generic error message is rarely sufficient.

Good error handling should explain what happened, whether funds moved, whether another action is required and where the transaction can be inspected.

In Web3, clarity during failure is just as important as speed during success.

Review the exchange rate and price impact.

Verify transaction details through a blockchain explorer.

Never assume that a successful wallet connection means a successful swap.

These checks are useful regardless of which exchange interface someone chooses.

A cryptocurrency swap is not merely a conversion between two token balances.

It is a coordinated process involving multiple systems, each with its own state, timing and security assumptions.

The best Web3 applications understand this distinction.

They do not just make the exchange button look simple. They make the underlying process understandable.

And that is a much more meaningful measure of good blockchain UX.

Forget Netflix, TV’s Greatest Classics Are Officially Getting a Second Life on Free Streaming

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In just a few short years, streaming has completely overhauled the type of entertainment we watch — and the way we watch it. Some streamers have done their best to hold onto the best parts of broadcast and cable, from clever algorithmic choices set to autoplay to Netflix's discontinued "Random Movie/TV Show" button. Still, some nostalgic users might prefer something more familiar, and Roku has them covered. The streaming giant has an app known as BackWhen TV, which mimics the interface of a cable channel guide.

Users scroll through different "channels" to see what's playing now and what's coming up in the next few hours. To really complete the experience, BackWhen TV is full of classic content going back decades, including TV shows, movies, and even ads. There's a lot to be said for the format offered by BackWhen TV, though it may not have been obvious until looking at it. This type of interface cuts down on decision fatigue, and subtly permits users to let their minds wander.

​​​​​​​These days, catching up on a streaming show can feel like homework as it demands your full attention, but BackWhen TV recalls a time when TV could be comforting background noise. It may also cut down on arguments about what to watch in a full house — an aspect that could be especially useful with the holidays approaching. Modern streaming clearly isn't going anywhere, but it's nice to know that something like BackWhen TV still exists when needed.

You can find BackWhen TV for free on any Roku device by searching for it on the home screen. It will even turn up if you search for "back when" or other variations of its name. The app is also available on some other Android TV devices. Once installed, it's easy to access, and it becomes indistinguishable from an old-school cable interface. Users must scroll through the channel selections to find the one they want, so it's more of a throwback to the 1990s and early 2000s than anything earlier. Still, it should conjure memories of channel-surfing for just about anyone who experienced it in recent memory.

Four of the largest communities on the planet, and four completely different reasons to belong to one. Here is yours, what it would give you, and the argument you would be having within a week.

You belong to the fandom that is really about comfort. Hogwarts works on people because it is a place rather than a plot, and the pull is not the wizard war so much as the corridors, the food, the weather and the sense that somebody is expecting you.

You belong to the deepest fandom on this list, the one where somebody has always read further than you and is quietly delighted to say so. Middle-earth rewards patience more than any other world in popular culture.

You belong to the loudest and largest tent of the four, the one where three generations are all watching the same thing and none of them agree about any of it. That disagreement is not a flaw in this fandom, it is the main activity.

You belong to the thinking fandom, the one that turns up for the argument rather than the explosion. Trek has always been a series of ethical problems in space, and the people who love it love being asked what they would have done.

As for the content itself, BackWhen TV is neatly organized into "channels" and programming blocks that call back to various time periods in the past. You won't see familiar brand-name networks like Nickelodeon or NBC, but channels are easy enough to figure out with names like "Cartoon Club" and "Hit Sitcoms." Some even bear the name of the show they're playing on loop, such as Betty Boop, Popeye, and Felix the Cat. There may be a slight preference for content aimed at a young audience, which makes sense for an app built on nostalgia, but there's a healthy mixture of shows for adults there as well.

BackWhen TV even preserves the old-school quality of programming, with movie channels listed as "VHS" showing full-screen films with a grainy look to them. There are a few channels showing old music videos and concert footage in the same style that MTV used in decades past. For those that want to go really deep into nostalgia, there are channels that only show old commercials, all organized by decade. They go back as far as the 1950s, and they may unlock dormant memories of jingles and slogans you thought you had forgotten.

There are plenty of uses for an app like BackWhen TV, but the upcoming family gatherings around the holidays might be the best scenario of all. The app could become nostalgic background noise for a busy kitchen, or a means of diffusing debates about what to watch while decorating a Christmas tree.

It may be comforting for older relatives who normally struggle with streaming interfaces, and it could even be useful for families with young children. Rather than giving a toddler free rein to explore streaming apps, this could be a way to let them enjoy screen time in moderation — depending on what's playing.

Of course, modern streaming isn't going anywhere, and it won't be easy to avoid this holiday season as so many classics are behind paywalls. However, for the times in between, WayBack TV may be the answer. The app is available now on Roku and other Android TV devices.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Michigan football coach Kyle Whittingham saves job with stunning UCLA upset in triple-OT

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Michigan got by far the biggest win of the Kyle Whittingham era on Saturday night.

It looked like the program would win in regulation, but UCLA tied the game in the final seconds of the fourth quarter.

MORE: Viral Georgia Tech couple goes through relationship turmoil as Jesse Palmer breaks it down

The two teams traded field goals in the first two overtimes before UCLA missed its two-point attempt in triple overtime, giving Michigan a chance to win. Michigan converted on its chance, thanks to a great pass from Bryce Underwood.

Wolverines fans stormed the field immediately after the two-point conversion.

The win improves Michigan's record to 6-5 ahead of next Saturday's game against Ohio State.

Michigan head coach Kyle Whittingham walks up the tunnel after 13-6 loss to Indiana in overtime at Michigan Stadium in Ann Arbor on Saturday, Oct. 24, 2026. Credit: USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

Before this win, not much had gone right for Michigan this season. The team was 5-5 through 10 games, and there was a real chance it wouldn't even make a bowl game this year.

However, Whittingham got everyone to buy in for four full quarters and then some to secure a win over one of the best teams in the country. Michigan's defense was especially exceptional in this game and didn't allow UCLA's offense to do much.

Offensively, Michigan did enough to win and stun the college football world. This is UCLA's first loss of the season and of the Bob Chesney era.

Michigan will go for its seventh win of the year next Saturday at noon ET on Fox.

This is what can happen if too many devices are sharing your home network

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It doesn’t take much to bring a home network to its knees.

Any home network, regardless of efficiency and performance, can get overburdened when enough devices are connected, each vying for its own slice of the pie. When that happens, it's pretty obvious, with the infamous buffering circle plaguing screens, laggy online gaming, endless download times and the apocalypse landing on your doorstep. It doesn't take much to reach this point either. A single high-end smartphone can eat up quite a bit of bandwidth all by itself.

There are two potential limitations to consider. The first is the internet connection coming into your home via your Internet Service Provider (ISP). The second is the Wi-Fi connection between your router and your devices. A faster internet plan won't fix a weak signal from a router tucked into an out-of-the-way corner. On the flip side, a faster router can't do much with a slow internet service plan.

The knee-jerk reaction is to replace your router, upgrade your internet service plan speed and place the router in a more efficient position. That may indeed help, but not everyone can run out and purchase a Wi-Fi 6 or 7 router and hassle their ISP for faster service and an accordingly higher bill. But you shouldn't have to, as long as you don't mind playing with the numbers.

There's no reliable rule that says "100 Mbps internet connection supports a specific number of devices." Instead, estimate how many devices can run simultaneously without bogging everything else down. Netflix, for example, recommends a stable connection of at least 15 Mbps for each 4K stream. Three streams represent about 45 Mbps of recommended capacity without accounting for any overhead throughout the home (even coffee pots connect to Wi-Fi nowadays).

No, this doesn't mean each stream constantly consumes exactly 15 Mbps. We're just using it as a reference number for the purposes of planning things out. If there are 10 devices in the home, each using 10 Mbps, that would equal 100 Mbps. In that case, a 100 Mbps download speed (FCC's minimum broadband requirement) would leave no room to spare if all 10 devices are up and running at the same time. In reality, of course, speeds fluctuate, but it's a helpful little math drill to run if you're trying to stay organized and keep that ridiculous buffering circle off your TV screen.

Upload speeds are important for things like video calls or security cameras uploading footage of squirrels fighting in the front yard. Upload speeds are generally much slower than download speeds, thanks to asymmetrical broadband with ISPs allocating most of the bandwidth to downloads. Once you understand the speeds and the average consumption of a single device, you can plan large downloads or other Wi-Fi-intensive tasks for less busy times.

For the vast majority of households, one router is more than enough. A good mesh system or efficiently placed access points will solve most problems. An additional router makes sense in very specific situations. For instance, if your ISP has no choice but to run your wires and install your gateway in the far corner of your home, running an Ethernet cable from the gateway to a router in the center of your home might work out great.

But your best bet is to start with what you already have, and add to it incrementally on an "as-needed" basis. Switch stationary devices (PCs, consoles, TVs, etc.) to Ethernet, if possible, removing their traffic from Wi-Fi, although their internet activity will still share your broadband connection. A router upgrade is an option if you need something that can efficiently manage more devices. Just remember that advertised device capacity is great, but it can't overcome bandwidth limitations on your ISP plan.

If you opt for a mesh system to complement an ISP gateway, follow the setup instructions closely and don't leave both devices performing routing duties without checking the configuration. If the gateway and mesh remain in their default routing modes, it could create double NAT, which might cause an issue with online gaming or port forwarding. It doesn't need to get too complicated because the end point boils down to addressing what is constraining your system. Crunch the numbers first; decide which direction to go second. You got this!

If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, Warren Buffett Says This Is the No. 1 Move All Investors Should Make

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The right strategy can protect your portfolio against even severe market volatility.

By Katie Brockman – Nov 21, 2026 at 7:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

It's been a lucrative year for the stock market, with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) notching more than two dozen record highs in 2026.

However, the headwinds have been coming in strong lately. Volatility within the tech sector has rattled Wall Street, and the tech-focused Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -5.28%) fell into correction territory after plunging nearly 12% from its recent peak, as of this writing.

The bad news for investors is that no bull market can last forever, and it's only a matter of time before we face a pullback. However, Warren Buffett has some timeless advice about how to prepare your portfolio for a potential market crash or recession.

Warren Buffett. Image source: Getty Images.

In late 1999, just four months before the dot-com bubble officially popped, Warren Buffett issued a warning to investors in an essay for Fortune. He emphasized that while new advancements in technology could transform society, that doesn't necessarily make them smart investments.

Using airlines as an example, Buffett noted that although air travel was revolutionary, 129 airlines had gone bankrupt over the previous 20 years. Televisions and radios fell into the same category as "glamorous businesses that dramatically changed our lives but concurrently failed to deliver rewards to U.S. investors."

The dot-com bubble eventually proved Buffett's point. While the internet went on to fundamentally transform the world, countless tech stocks crashed and burned in the early 2000s.

"The key to investing is not assessing how much an industry is going to affect society, or how much it will grow," Buffett explained, "but rather determining the competitive advantage of any given company and, above all, the durability of that advantage."

While it's impossible to say how the market will fare in the coming months, the dot-com bubble serves as a valuable lesson. Artificial intelligence (AI) as a technology could be revolutionary, but that doesn't mean every AI stock will be a good investment. Inevitably, some stocks will flounder during the next downturn.

The best move investors can make now is to load up on a wide variety of quality stocks with robust business fundamentals. While all stocks are vulnerable to short-term volatility, those with strong competitive advantages are the most likely to recover and deliver long-term growth.

Healthy companies will likely thrive over time. Although the S&P 500 lost almost half of its value between 2000 and 2002, the index soared by more than 200% in the two decades following the dot-com bubble.

If we're headed toward a similar bear market, not all stocks will recover. But healthy companies will fuel decades of growth, and the investors who look past the hype and focus on quality stocks will reap the biggest rewards.

Should You Invest $1,000 in Bloom Energy Right Now?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bloom stock is plunging, but business is booming. Is this the buying opportunity of a lifetime?

By Steven Porrello – Nov 21, 2026 at 7:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Bloom Energy ›

Earlier this year, things seemed to be going exactly right for fuel-cell manufacturer Bloom Energy (BE -15.30%).

It's not hard to see why. The race to build AI data centers had stimulated such an enormous appetite for electricity that developers, facing grid bottlenecks and tight construction timelines, simply couldn't afford to wait years for traditional power grid connections.

In that context, new orders for Bloom's fuel-cell systems were coming in hot: First Oracle (ORCL -8.48%) contracted for 1.2 gigawatts (GW) of capacity, then Brookfield Asset Management (BAM -2.53%) expanded its financing framework with Bloom for AI projects from $5 billion to a whopping $25 billion.

Both were significant developments for Bloom's business, and Wall Street absolutely agreed: The stock soared to an all-time high of roughly $350, more than quadrupling in value from where it started the year.

If you take a look at Bloom's stock chart today, however, you'll see a different picture: It has fallen from its high to roughly $168 per share.

That raises the obvious question: What the heck is happening? And, more importantly, should you take this as an occasion to buy Bloom stock?

For starters, Bloom is a fuel cell manufacturer. In simple terms, it makes equipment that let data centers and other customers generate electricity on-site rather than waiting years to connect to the grid. These fuel cells are also a source of (relatively) clean power: Instead of burning fuel, they use an electrochemical reaction to convert fuel (such as natural gas) into electricity. It's a pretty neat concept, and it is especially useful for those who need round-the-clock power.

As mentioned, Bloom stock soared earlier this year as sales of Bloom boxes went through the roof. This hasn't changed. Indeed, third-quarter sales surged 138% year over year to $1.24 billion, prompting management (yet again!) to raise full-year revenue guidance to between $4.4 billion and $4.6 billion. For perspective, its full-year 2025 revenue was about $2 billion.

What has changed, however, is the environment, or more specifically: Wall Street's willingness to pay sky-high valuations for companies associated with the AI boom. Investors have become increasingly concerned about the billions being poured into AI infrastructure, and Bloom, whose future growth is tied to AI spending, has found itself caught in this broader wave of bearish sentiment.

The irony, of course, is that the very numbers that once sent Bloom's stock soaring are no longer enough to reassure investors. But why is that?

If I could capture that shift in perspective in an image, it would be this: Bloom's breathtaking growth this year is like a star's light. It's brilliant to look at, but it tells us more about the past than it does the future. It tells us just how bullish data center developers were on AI infrastructure earlier this year, but it doesn't necessarily indicate how Bloom will fare if that spending slows or stalls.

In other words, Bloom's extraordinary financial results could be a lagging indicator of an AI spending frenzy that is fading, while its stock price reflects anxiety about what will come next.

So, given that Wall Street is concerned about Bloom's future sales, should you reverse course and buy?

Personally, I think this is a great opportunity to pick up Bloom stock. The reaction to concerns over data center construction strikes me as a bit overdone. Even if AI spending slows, the underlying problem Bloom is solving -- an electricity grid struggling to keep up with rising demands -- isn't going away anytime soon. The stock might be volatile today, but for those willing to hold through the volatility, I think today's sell-off offers an attractive opportunity to buy a company with long-term growth potential.

Your PC's USB port can charge your phone, even when it's turned off — Here's how

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Your USB ports might have a hidden "Always On" mode that charges your phone with the PC shut down.

Almost all PC USB ports supply enough current to charge a device, but not all continue to do so when the PC is shut down. This convenient feature can also be confusing, since each brand tends to have its own terminology. For example, Lenovo calls it an "Always On" port while Dell uses the term PowerShare. Others stick with icon labels, like a battery symbol or a lightning bolt. The best way to know for sure is to dig into your PC's manual, confirm the markings and learn how to enable it.

Depending on the system, USB standby power might be on by default or be part of an energy-saving setting that cuts power when the PC is off. Your desktop or motherboard documentation should have this information. Fortunately, testing the feature is fairly simple: plug your phone in, turn the PC off and see if your phone continues charging.

If it stops charging, it's safe to assume the setting is off, which you can rectify in the BIOS or UEFI on most Windows PCs. (If your PC never supported the feature, changing settings won't help.) Restart the PC and press the key the startup screen tells you to (usually F2, Del or F10). Look under Advanced, Power or Chipset for a USB power delivery or Always On charging option and enable it. If you see ErP (Energy-related Products) or Deep Sleep settings, make sure they're disabled. Save and exit.

You don't need a computer science or software engineering degree to do this, just a little patience as you wait for the PC to boot up and press the right key. The menu can be confusing at times, but you're unlikely to brick your PC.

For starters, energy-saving features may get in the way. Anything that uses standby power will conflict with firmware settings that cut power to reduce consumption. For instance, if ErP Ready is enabled in select ASUS NUC PCs, USB standby power during shutdown or hibernation is disabled. Check your system's instructions before changing anything.

You also may not have to turn off every single energy-saving feature just to charge your phone when your PC is off. Keep in mind that changing the energy-saving settings won't add post-shutdown charging if the system never supported it to begin with.

Laptops are an entirely different case, since they often come with battery reserve limitations. Depending on the model, USB charging may stop when the laptop's battery hits a reserve threshold. Laptop charging features may depend on an AC adapter after shutdown, and that's a potential restriction that shouldn't be confused with a desktop's standby power settings. Make sure your USB plug is completely in the port and that the cable is reliable, not one that's been sitting in an attic box for the last quarter century.

In this context, "Always On" refers to power availability, but that's not the same thing as "charging only." A supported port can continue to supply power while the computer is off, though the wording shouldn't be taken literally. Firmware settings can disable that power, and the behavior may be different between ports on the same system.

Power and data transfers are also different capabilities. A phone charging while the PC is shut down doesn't mean you can transfer photos from your phone or copy files to it. The specific feature here is all about electricity available via the USB connection.

Another problem is the lack of consistency in names, setup processes and USB port symbols. One system may offer a dedicated USB power setting while another's ports depend on power-saving configurations. Even within a single brand, capabilities and terminology can differ, and manufacturers implement "Always On" USB in drastically different ways.

For example, "Always On" could mean the feature is active after shutdown, during hibernation or while your PC is asleep. It could mean two of them, just one or all three. The feature may come with symbols, color coding, terminology or fine print in a manual, but not necessarily all of the above.

Why Beetee Latier From The Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping Looks So Familiar

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This article contains spoilers for "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping."

Prequel film "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping" reveals the details of how Haymitch Abernathy (Joseph Zada, Woody Harrelson in the original films) won the 50th Hunger Games ... and lost everything else in his life, turning him into a miserable man using alcohol to cope. Yet "The Hunger Games: Catching Fire" revealed Haymitch wasn't merely the burnout he first appeared to be, but a key member of the rebellion against the Capitol.

"Sunrise on the Reaping" shows the beginning of that, particularly Haymitch's early alliance with Plutarch Heavensbee (Jesse Plemons, filling in for the late Philip Seymour Hoffman). Younger versions of several past Hunger Games victors first introduced in "Catching Fire" also show up in "Sunrise on the Reaping," including the tech wizard Beetee Latier (Kelvin Harrison Jr. doing an uncanny vocal impression of original Beetee actor Jeffrey Wright).

Beetee has already been caught rebelling against the Capitol. As punishment, his own son Ampert (Percy Daggs IV) was reaped into the Hunger Games. Beetee and Haymitch collaborate as part of a conspiracy to have Haymitch disrupt the arena's mechanical systems and, hopefully, sabotage the Games once he's inside. When Haymitch does so, but the Games persist and, when the conspiracy is discovered, he takes the fall. The Capitol has already done the worst thing it could to Beetee by taking his son, and as a victor, he's too valuable and visible a symbol to kill.

"Hunger Games" fans already know this isn't the last we've seen of Beetee, and this probably isn't the first you've seen of his actor who plays him. Born in New Orleans in 1994, Harrison is the son of two musicians, but he chose acting as his primary creative path.

Harrison's film career began with incidental parts in "12 Years A Slave" and "Ender's Game" in 2013, which were followed by his first starring role in 2017's post-apocalyptic horror film "It Comes At Night." Harrison played the character of Travis, son of Paul (Joel Edgerton) and Sarah (Carmen Ejogo).

In 2018, Harrison appeared in "Monster," Netflix's adaptation of Walter Dean Myers's 1999 novel about a Black honor student accused of murder. The following year, Harrison starred in a surprisingly similar film: the psychodrama "Luce," playing a former child soldier from Eritrea adopted by a wealthy white couple (Naomi Watts and Tim Roth). Luce is a straight-A student, popular athlete, and seemingly on his way to great things ... until he writes an essay that appears to defend radical political violence as a valid means of change.

Having proved himself with those leading roles, Harrison has continued making himself into one of Hollywood's most prolific young actors even before joining "The Hunger Games." Several of his prominent roles have been in historical dramas: In 2021, he played Black Panther leader Fred Hampton in 2021's "The Trial of the Chicago 7," and in 2022's "Elvis" he played Blues musician B.B. King. Harrison also played Martin Luther King Jr. in Season 4 of National Geographic series "Genius," opposite Aaron Pierre (now known as John Stewart from "Lanterns") as Malcolm X.

But that's not all Harrison can do. He played Christian de Neuvillette in Joe Wright's 2021 musical film "Cyrano," adapting the 1897 French play by Edmond Rostand. In 2024 "Lion King" prequel "Mufasa," he voiced Scar. Being in company with Jeremy Irons and Chiwetel Ejiofor is pretty impressive for an actor Harrison's age.

"The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping" is now in theaters.

I Can’t Get Over Rob Lowe Explaining Why It’s Easier To Fly ‘100 Americans’ To Ireland Than Film The Floor In LA

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

I'm Loving Netflix's Win The Mall (And Haley Baylee), But There's One Thing I'd Like To See More Of

These (Alleged) Reality TV Lies From AGT To Bar Rescue Will Have You Appalled

I Just Found Out How Much NCIS Is Actually Worth, And I Am Flabbergasted

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I knew it had become increasingly rare for Hollywood productions to actually be filmed in Los Angeles, but Rob Lowe’s stats for The Floor really put things into perspective. The host of the Fox game show, which is on the 2026 TV schedule this fall season, just got real about why it’s filmed all the way in Ireland rather than at the network’s own lot. And, wow!

Ever since The Floor premiered back in 2023, the game show featuring 100 contestants, a giant LED floor grid and a $250,000 grand prize has been made in Ireland. When Lowe was asked why he shoots it overseas, he said this:

When people want to know how bad the economics are in California, and Los Angeles in particular in terms of the industry that created this town. It is more cost-efficient to find 100 Americans and bring them to Ireland and shoot the game show in Ireland than it is to shoot in the soundstages [on the Fox lot] right outside the window of the offices where we’re trying to figure out what we’re going to do next season.

Yeah, this really illustrates the rough situation in LA right now. When I looked deeper into it, I found out that there are a few factors that probably played into The Floor deciding to go to Ireland rather than stay in Los Angeles. One is the rigid labor regulations in Los Angeles that just don’t apply in Ireland. Another is how much more expensive it would be for the production to board the one hundred contestants during the shoots. And then, there’s the competitive tax incentives Ireland has in place to encourage productions to be there. Lowe continued with these words:

There’s a new California initiative that did pass to help bring production back into LA. And then, there's also a national one that people are talking about. But, that would still only make it competitive with some of the other [markets]. I mean, it’s 40% less expensive, 40. It’s a lot of money.

It sounds like The Floor just wouldn’t be able to exist if it were shot in Los Angeles. There are a number of TV shows that currently shoot in the City of Angels, like Dancing With The Stars, The Pitt, The Rookie, 911, Shrinking and Abbott Elementary, and some incentives in California are starting to help repair the market. However, as Lowe also shared while on Mostly Sheenius, there’s a long way to go before it becomes cost-efficient again. As he put it:

It is definitely trending up, but it is trending up from the basement of where it used to be.

Tons of Los Angeles TV shows recently moved to new locations, such as Shark Tank and American Idol moving to Atlanta and The Masked Singer to New Jersey. It’s also been reported lately (per Deadline) that America’s Got Talent is scouting a new home outside of its longtime home in Pasadena, California.

The Floor airs new episodes on Wednesdays (with the finale airing on December 2). If you’re looking to catch up on the new season, you can do so now with either a Disney+ subscription and/or a Hulu subscription. Lowe’s explanation of why The Floor shoots in Ireland kind of blows my mind, but as a Los Angeles resident, I totally see how expensive it is over here, too. Hopefully some of these incentives will change things because so much of the workforce over here relies on the entertainment industry.

10 things you should never do on your Android phone

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Avoid major mistakes that make your Android phone less secure and less convenient to use.

If you've used Android for a while, you probably know some of its most important rules. But even with an OS that's been around for more than 15 years, myths and outdated assumptions still persist. Or you might be new to Android and not totally sure how to best take care of your device.

Either way, Android doesn't come with a complete user manual, so we've compiled some of the mistakes you shouldn't make on an Android phone. They include ignoring security features and trying advanced functions without understanding them. Follow these, and your Android phone should last years in good condition.

Why it's bad: Software updates fix known vulnerabilities. Patches in a software update are often responsive, meaning they protect against attacks that are happening, not just theoretical threats. Also, if you use high-security apps like banking and wallet tools, those services won't work if your phone gets too behind on security updates.

Who says so: Security vulnerabilities in Android are reported to Google, and Google shares this information confidentially with Android device makers. You can read about what threats Google considers critical on its Android Security page.

Google doesn't share this information publicly for 90 days, giving vendors time to create proper patches and preventing would-be attackers from taking advantage of the details before people have a chance to install patches. There are a lot of players here: Google is responsible for Android software, then every phone maker adds software to Android devices that needs to be patched. Chip makers like MediaTek and Qualcomm also need to patch the drivers that allow those chips to function properly.

What's the best practice: Install every security update as soon as it appears. Also, open the Google Play Store and make sure to update apps regularly — automatic updates are best.

Why it's bad: When your phone is running too many processes at once, it slows down. That seems obvious, but what's actually happening is your phone is shuttling apps between three internal components: storage, RAM and a special partition of RAM called ZRAM made to quickly store and recall active processes.

When your RAM fills up, it starts compressing processes so it can cram them into the ZRAM partition. When there's no more room in ZRAM for compressed data, your phone enters a state called memory thrashing, where it repeatedly loads data from the ZRAM and then compresses it back in an ongoing cycle. This causes your phone to slow down and heat up as the CPU drains power.

Who says so: The Android Open Source Project offers technical details on the so-called "low memory killer daemon" (the software process that stuffs data into ZRAM), and the problems caused when you let that memory fill up with extraneous data pages.

What's the best practice: Restart your phone regularly to clear the RAM and ZRAM. When you're done with a heavy app like a game, swipe to close it from the app switcher menu (though you don't need to do this constantly for all apps). Finally, uninstall apps that you no longer use to make sure they aren't running processes you don't need. On Android, even if you don't open an app, it may still run and use your RAM in the background.

As another part of core maintenance, make sure to keep enough space free on your Android phone. Running it with maxed-out storage means it has no room for temporary caches and other "helper" data.

Why it's bad: There is no easier way for bad actors to take advantage of security flaws than from apps that come from outside Google's Play Store garden. One common practice is for bad apps to take advantage of your phone's Accessibility controls, which grant privileges for copying your phone's screen and running apps in the background. Google is cautious about approving accessibility access on the Play Store, which is why bad apps are often found on third-party sites.

Who says so: According to Google's security blog, Google Play Protect identified more than 13 million malicious apps from outside the Play Store in 2024 — and those are just the ones the service was able to detect. The standards for Android app security are set by the App Defense Alliance, part of Linux, which forms the foundation of Android's code.

What's the best practice: If you must install apps from outside Google Play, make sure you 100 percent trust the source. A first-party developer you trust, or a security-conscious third-party app market like F-Droid, is better than a random forum or Reddit page. Always use caution outside the Google Play Store.

Why it's bad: If you're asked to disable Google Play Protect for any reason, that's a major red flag. Usually, a scammer will try to convince you to download a phony app by pretending to be your bank or another trusted source. When you try to install the new app, you get a warning from Play Protect, which the scammer tells you to disregard. Never ignore this warning.

Who says so: Play Protect has been a part of Android for almost 10 years, and it gets constant updates that make it more stringent. It's the primary line of defense against dangerous apps you try to install.

What's the best practice: Never disable Google Play Protect, but don't trust it blindly either. Researchers like Bitdefender claim to have found apps that made it to the Play Store and still pose a risk, so you still need to be careful about what you install.

Why it's bad: In the early days of Android, there were good reasons to obtain root access to your phone. You could enable features that were inexplicably turned off, like network tethering on Verizon's early Android devices. It allows you to remove bloatware installed by your mobile carrier, or even load an OS like CyanogenMod that was better than what Samsung or Google provided. Those days are over.

Rooting a phone triggers failures in Google's Play Integrity API, and that's a break that can't heal. You won't be able to use banking or financial apps or any apps that rely on your phone's Strong integrity, a tag that disappears when you obtain root access to your system. Having root access means mistakes or malicious apps can cause much more damage.

Who says so: Even the US government says this is a bad idea. The National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) warns against rooting your mobile device, listing rooting and jailbreaking a phone as a threat in its Mobile Threat Catalogue.

What's the best practice: There is not enough reason to root your primary Android phone today if you aren't a software developer. Phone features are no longer restricted based on which carrier sold you the phone. And phone makers let you remove (or at least hide) the bloat — you can even remove Google apps that used to be permanently stuck in your app drawer.

If you have an old device that no longer gets updates, rooting and installing a custom ROM can bring new life to it, but the trade-offs aren't worth it on an in-support daily phone.

Why it's bad: Say your new Android phone came with a bunch of apps you don't want. The Settings menu lets you disable them so they disappear, but they're still taking up space and you can't let it go.

So, you found a YouTube video that shows you how to turn on USB Debugging, which lets you manage your phone's files from your PC. You figure this isn't a problem, since you aren't installing anything from a third party or handing your phone to a stranger.

Except those instructions often skip an important final step: remember to turn USB Debugging off. Otherwise, your phone is more vulnerable to attacks using a computer. While Android requires you to authorize the connection when you connect to any PC for the first time, you might not realize what you're doing, or someone could trick you into handing over access. That's why it's important to watch the prompts when connecting to an unknown USB port (more on this below).

There are lots of features on Android that can give you advanced access to your system without being dangerous, as long as you're careful to turn them off when you're done.

Who says so: Google is enthusiastic about Android users trying Developer Mode, but admits "it's essential to understand the implications of each setting." That's a massive understatement, since these options can cause problems if you don't know what they do.

What's the best practice: If you dabble in the Android cauldron, be sure to put out the fire. Disable USB Debugging and any other features that you turned on once the task is done. Follow instructions closely, and don't mess with features you don't truly understand.

Why it's bad: When you access an open Wi-Fi network, you have no guarantees of privacy. Anyone else who joins the network can use free analyzer tools to see what's happening. Almost every modern website uses HTTPS, meaning information you send to a site (like login credentials) is encrypted. But someone snooping on the network would still be able to see which sites you're accessing.

Even if the network seems trustworthy, it's hard to be sure. For example, say you use the password on the wall to connect to a cafe network. Someone could have set up a fake network with the same name and password combination, and your device won't know the difference. It looks for the strongest connection, which might be the person next to you with a sketchy hotspot and Wi-Fi sniffer, not the router mounted above the ceiling.

Who says so: The US Government's Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency said public Wi-Fi networks are one of the biggest threats for high-risk security attacks, though this was in 2012 before HTTPS was as common as it is now. Technology departments at universities like Vanderbilt continue to warn of the risks and how to mitigate them.

What's the best practice: Use a VPN for another layer of encryption on untrusted networks. If you must use public Wi-Fi without a VPN, avoid working with sensitive info like bank details. And if a site doesn't use HTTPS, leave it for when you're at home.

Why it's bad: When you plug into a USB port, you are often connecting your phone to both a power source and a data connection. Your phone will prompt you to trust a new device for data connections, which is where the risk comes in. If you grant that access, an attacker could steal your photos, load malware onto your phone or intercept communications, among other harmful actions.

Who says so: The FCC has issued warnings against plugging your phone into unknown USB ports. While no reports of so-called "juice jacking" attacks have happened in the real world, the technique has been demonstrated multiple times at hacking events (first at the Black Hat security conference in 2013). There's even a USB cable called the O.MG sold by an InfoSec testing company, Hak5, that contains the necessary hardware to pull off a juice jacking attack — for testing purposes, of course.

What's the best practice: When you plug your phone in at an airport, don't accept any pop-up notices that appear. If possible, use a proper wall outlet or a portable charger to eliminate the small theoretical risk of these attacks.

Why it's bad: Apps often ask for more information than they need. Does a calculator app need access to your photos? Does a flashlight app need to use the microphone? Those are obvious examples of permissions you should be critical of. More important are Accessibility permissions, which allow an app to monitor everything on your screen, monitor your SMS messages (for 2FA codes, for example) and even control other apps without your knowledge. That sketchy to-do list app could open your banking app, change your password and have its way with your accounts, if it has proper accessibility access.

Another permission to watch is Display over other apps. This lets Facebook float Messenger chat heads on top of your home screen and other apps, but could also let a bogus app overlay a fake OK button that gives permission to further abuse. It may look like you're tapping Agree in the Google Play Store, when the button is actually a separate app that will install a nasty payload.

Who says so: Android takes permissions seriously: the latest versions of Android revoke all permissions from apps you haven't used in a long time, just to be safe. Google tells developers to ask for permissions incrementally, and only when a feature is needed, so you have context for why the app is asking.

What's the best practice: You know to be careful with microphone and camera access, but also be aware when an app asks for any unusual permissions. Regularly review your permissions and remove any that are unnecessary (or uninstall apps you never use).

Why it's bad: Lithium batteries hate extremes. When one is always kept at 100 percent, it increases the wear on internal components and reduces the total charge the battery can hold over time, according to Samsung. That's why modern iOS and Android versions come with battery management tools that keep your phone around 80 percent while you charge overnight, only topping up to 100 percent just before you wake up.

You should also avoid using cheap, off-brand USB cables for charging. Your phone came with a high-quality USB cable that includes a controller chip to make sure the device gets a steady voltage. Cheap USB cables often lack that chip, which can cause your battery to overheat. At worst, it can cause internal electrical damage, though any excess heat is bad for your battery's chemistry. It speeds up reactions and can cause the battery to degrade faster.

Who says so: Most phone makers have a warning page about batteries, with battery utilities recommending that you only charge your phone to 80 percent under normal circumstances. This will extend the longevity of the battery, which is a good idea if you plan on keeping your phone for many years.

What's the best practice: Let Android do its job. Your phone has software that helps manage battery, both for day-to-day life and overall longevity. Don't force your phone to charge past Android's recommended limit all the time.

FBI reportedly investigating apparent hack of its criminal database

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The FBI is investigating an apparent breach of its criminal information database, US media reports, citing two people briefed on the issue.

The National Crime Information Center (NCIC) database is used by federal and local law enforcement to share information on warrants and background checks.

It would not be the first time hackers have targeted the FBI, the domestic intelligence and security service of the US. The agency did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.

When asked about the breach in a Fox News interview on Saturday, President Donald Trump said he had "just heard" about the hack. "I will say that it's not good. I don't like that," he said.

"But maybe we'll learn things that we would've never learned otherwise," Trump added.

This apparent breach of FBI database comes after a string of tech outages which hit banking, air traffic control and other sectors in the US last week. Officials have said they were not caused by cyber-attacks, but the blackouts have sparked scrutiny from lawmakers.

White House National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross was fired this week, with Trump saying he was not good enough.

Dan Lorhmann, a former analyst for the National Security Agency (NSA), told the BBC News Channel that the NCIC's data included "critical information relating to nationwide criminal activity" and needed to be protected.

He added that while US security agencies including the FBI have "an amazing ability" to keep such information safe" and are "globally respected", the level of cyber-attacks is rising globally.

"There's a lot of different reasons why that's happening but I do have faith in our experts in government," Lorhmann said.

In September, the FBI said it was investigating an apparent breach by a cyber-crime group ShinyHunters, which claimed it had stolen sensitive information on all bureau staff - around 38,000 people.

After that hack, one former FBI agent told the BBC he feared for the safety of current employees, whose personal information could soon become available online to criminals and hostile nation-state hackers.

In a separate development, NBC reported an apparent cyberattack against the US Department of Agriculture (USDA).

Employees were informed on Thursday that its farm payment system had been targeted, NBC said.

The USDA has not publicly commented on the issue.

John Madden Famously Quit The Raiders. One Madden Star Had To Film This On Day 1

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.
