Sprocket Security Named a Leader in GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One year after debuting as a Challenger and Outperformer, Sprocket earns perfect scores on four criteria as it pairs expert pentesters with a fleet of AI agents.

MADISON, Wis., Nov. 20, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Sprocket Security has been named a Leader in the GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service (PTaaS) v5. Sprocket placed as a Leader in the report's Agile Optimizers scorecards and earned perfect marks on test launch efficiency, attack surface integration, rules of engagement controls, and the testing audit trail.

The placement marks a clear step up from 2025, when GigaOm recognized Sprocket as a Challenger and Outperformer. In the year between, Sprocket expanded its capabilities with a fleet of AI agents that work alongside its pentesters, extending how much of the attack surface gets tested and how quickly.

Sprocket Security has been named a Leader in the GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service (PTaaS) v5.

This year's report, authored by GigaOm analyst Chris Ray, maps four buyer profiles to four quadrants. It frames five market themes around the questions buyers should ask before signing: who performs the test, how it reaches internal networks, and what evidence an auditor will accept?

"Sprocket Security earns its Leader position by pairing AI testing agents with human validation of every finding, and wrapping both in machine-enforced scope, a customer kill switch and a checksummed audit trail, so its testing keeps pace with a changing attack surface without giving up control," Ray shared.

Sprocket's top scores reflect how the platform runs day to day:

Test launch efficiency (5.0): Teams scope and start testing without weeks of kickoff calls and paperwork.

Attack surface integration (5.0): Testing follows the attack surface as it changes, so new assets get tested as they appear.

Rules of engagement controls (5.0): Customers set what gets tested, when, and how, with guardrails built into the platform.

Testing audit trail (5.0): Every action is logged, giving security teams and auditors a record of what was tested and what was found.

Sprocket was founded because point-in-time pentests couldn't keep up with how companies change. Continuous testing closed part of that gap. Sprocket's AI agents close more of it, running alongside its pentesters on the Sprocket platform:

Apex: an agentic pentester for unauthenticated web applications, mapping each app and testing its own theories about where it might break.

Link: an agentic pentester for authenticated web applications, testing what sits behind the login.

Pinion: an agentic pentester for internal networks, falling the attack paths worth chasing.

Sprocket's pentesters direct the work and validate what the agents find, so customers get more coverage without trading away the judgment of an experienced tester.

"A year ago GigaOm called us a Challenger. Today we are a Leader, and agentic innovation is why. We put a fleet of AI agents to work next to our pentesters for all types of testing, and the results show up in these scores," said Casey Cammilleri, CEO and founder of Sprocket Security. "Buyers are asking who is actually testing, whether it reaches the parts of the network attackers go after, and whether the results will hold up with an auditor. Our answer is experienced pentesters with agents that extend their reach, on a platform that shows its work."

Organizations that work with Sprocket Security get:

Sprocket will keep expanding its AI fleet and testing coverage. Every new agent is held to Sprocket's Safety Framework for Autonomous Offensive Security Agents, which requires agents to stay within scope, stop at proof, log every action, and let humans step in at any point. The aim is to give buyers clear answers to the questions GigaOm says to ask: who is testing, how far the testing reaches, and what evidence it leaves behind.

Security leaders can access a complimentary copy of the GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service v5 at https://www.sprocketsecurity.com/gigaom.

Unlike legacy penetration testing, Sprocket's continuous approach delivers real-time insights and adaptive security, giving organizations a security posture that reflects today, not their last test.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang accused of decades of bullying and workplace abuse in new investigation

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Public humiliation reportedly considered a "rite of passage".

An investigation into the workplace behavior of Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang, who resigned recently after a series of extramarital affairs came to light, has uncovered decades of harassment, verbal employee abuse and "cultish" behaviour at the company which has long played a major role in PC gaming.

Following Huang's departure, The New York Times has published a report in which "more than a dozen" current and former Nvidia employees discussed their experiences with the former CEO. Speaking on conditions of anonymity, employees alleged Jensen Huang would verbally berate them, reportedly going as far as to say he'd rip their arms off, rip their heart out and stomp on it, or rip their head off and "pee on your brain".

The report includes numerous examples of Huang's alleged abusive behaviour, which apparently stretches back years before Nvidia became the one of the most valuable companies in the world - years before the AI boom. It's claimed Huang on one occasion went around a room of managers telling them he'd kill them. Then, when he reached a person in their 70s, said everyone hoped they'd drop dead.

Huang allegedly referred to these bouts of public humiliation as a "rite of passage" at Nvidia, when questioned by an internal HR employee whom he later fired for not being a "right fit". Huan's stance was apparently to test whether a person could handle the culture there, which is described in the report as "cultish".

It's claimed Huang's influence at Nvidia has meant other senior managers have followed his lead, including his daughter Madison, Nvidia's chief of staff, who it's said often yells at staff, including executives more highly ranked than her.

A spokesperson for Huang denied the allegations in response to a request for comment by the New York Times. The spokesperson, John Fisher, said the claims in the report were "fabrications that are now impossible to disprove specifically, and the thousands of Nvidia employees who continue to speak out for Jensen show they’re false".

Huang resigned from his role as Nvidia CEO earlier this month after a series of extramarital affairs were unearthed - one with a person US intelligence believes to be a Chinese spy, and two others with Nvidia employees. The chair of Nvidia's board, Harvey C. Jones, reportedly said earlier this week that the company "does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO".

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Finally, law-abiding Switch 2 users can rest easy

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

SmileBasic 4, one of the key pieces of software being used to jailbreak Switch 2 consoles, is back on the Nintendo eShop. While the console's firmware has long since been updated to stop known jailbreak exploits, SmileBasic 4 itself has now also been patched.

SmileBasic is a line of programming tools which developer SmileBoom has released for various Nintendo platforms, allowing users to code their own simple programs and games. It's also opened multiple backdoors for jailbreak exploits over the years, first on 3DS, and then on Switch 2.

Technically, SmileBasic 4 is a Switch 1 game, but AI-backed hackers found a way to use it in order to jailbreak Switch 2 consoles, allowing the machines to run code not approved by Nintendo. That would allow users to, in theory, run homebrew applications, but as far as I can tell, the exploit had not yet been extended to allow people to play pirated games.

Nonetheless, Nintendo acted quickly to delist SmileBasic 4 from the eShop and to update the console's firmware to address the exploit at a system level. That's a necessary step, too, as SmileBasic 4 was not the only application that could be used to exploit it. Notably, Civilization 7, a game that is available on cartridge and thus impervious to delistings and software updates, could also be used for the jailbreak.

Even so, it seems that SmileBasic 4 itself has been updated to ensure that a similar exploit never happens again. As Twitter user MarioMasta64 notes, the software itself has gotten a new update post-reinstatement aimed at closing the hole used for the jailbreak. The application's demo – which could also be used to exploit it – has also been updated, but it has not yet been relisted on the eShop.

The makers behind Assassin’s Creed are going all in on generative AI while letting human talent go

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Every time news comes out about the French video game publisher/developer Ubisoft, it’s either something gamers are pleasantly surprised by or something entirely negative that doesn’t come as a shock.

It was nice to learn that studio franchises like Assassin’s Creed, The Division and Rayman were either getting content expansions or new series installments altogether. But it was equally disappointing to learn about the 2026 cancellation of several in-development projects, including the troubled remake of Prince of Persia: The Sands of Time. Gamers were ecstatic to hear that Ubisoft halted any future development in NFTs and Web3 gaming. But now they’ll probably be less than enthused all over again, now that Ubisoft is shifting its next big tech focus to generative AI.

After releasing its latest earnings report, Ubisoft has announced its intentions to go all-in on incorporating generative AI technologies into video game development with a new studio. But with that news comes the company’s latest round of layoffs, which resulted in hundreds of its studio’s game developers losing their roles at Ubisoft.

With both events happening so close to each other, it’s easy to see why gamers are questioning Ubisoft’s latest moves.

Ubisoft made it known that they’re making a big move towards playable generative AI experiences when it highlighted the new in-house studio behind its efforts in its most recent earnings report.

“Motherbrain, the team behind Teammates, the Group’s first playable generative AI experience, has been established as a dedicated subsidiary developing proprietary technology for new forms of gameplay. Its current focus is on new player experiences, smart NPCs with memory and AI game masters that manage large-scale systemic worlds,” the company stated. “It aims to serve creators globally, both within Ubisoft’s Creative House ecosystem and beyond, and form partnerships with selected technology actors.”

Ubisoft CEO Yves Guillemot commented on the creation of Motherbrain, noting that it is “the team behind our first playable generative AI experience Teammates, as a dedicated subsidiary developing proprietary technologies for gameplay to empower creators and open up new possibilities for richer, more immersive and engaging player experiences.”

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

Those experiences will focus on utilizing more intelligent non-playable characters with stronger memory and AI game masters that properly manage large-scale in-game worlds.

Motherbrain’s Teammates has been in development for a good while now and was recently showcased to members of the gaming media in a playable state. The people who got their hands on it refer to it as an immersive first-person shooter where players are joined by two robotic AI companions that they can order around by issuing real-time voice commands.

The game’s narrative director, Virginie Mosser, looked to ease the fears of gamers who are wary of generative AI being implemented in video games and AI tools being used to make them.

“At first, I had the same concerns as many others,” Mosser noted. “But I discovered that it’s the exact opposite of removing the human from the process. I still write the story and character personalities, but instead of fixed lines, we create these kinds of fences that let NPCs improvise within the world but stay within the boundaries of the lore and motivations we have given them. They can improvise, but we still set the rules and direct the story and characters.”

Gamers still have reason to give Ubisoft the side eye about its big generative AI video game development plans, especially since the company was so quick to abandon its trend-chasing foray into the world of NFTs and Web3 gaming. The most recent round of layoffs at the company, which resulted in the loss of 166 roles from its Montreal and Quebec studios, has also given gamers another reason to be skeptical about Ubisoft’s AI game-making initiative.

Even though Ubisoft enacted these layoffs “to ensure our resources are aligned with current production priorities and business needs,” it’s not out of the realm of possibility to question its true motives for its latest job cuts.

Has Ubisoft decided to pour major investments into generative AI game development at the cost of sacrificing its human talent? Will more layoffs occur in the near future as the company strengthens its AI game development efforts? Can gamers expect to hear about more AI tool usage implemented in the creation of future entries in Ubisoft’s biggest franchises? And if Motherbrain’s Teammates bombs, will Ubisoft quickly leave generative AI video game development behind and go on to the next big tech fad?

As someone who’s always chatting with gamers and critics who cover the medium, I always hear how they could do without any sort of AI being utilized in the video game creation process. And they especially don’t want to hear AI voices emanating from their favorite games and replacing human ones.

That last sentiment is evidenced by the overwhelmingly negative reactions to Embark Studios (the development studio behind The Finals) replacing its human esports broadcasters with in-game AI commentators. Ubisoft has experienced some major ups and downs as of late—their major bet on generative AI game development has a high chance of producing another loss if their latest endeavors don’t work as well as intended.

Today, Elton focuses on making AI more accessible by breaking down complex topics into useful, easy-to-understand insights for a wide range of readers.

Prediction: This Is What Will Happen to Micron’s Stock Price in December

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The memory specialist has taken a beating in recent months, but it could get a nice shot in the arm when it releases its quarterly results next month.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 20, 2026 at 12:18PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Micron Technology ›

Micron Technology (MU -12.71%) has made investors significantly richer in 2026, with shares of the memory specialist up 123% as of this writing. However, the stock has run into rough weather in the second half of the year.

Micron stock has lost 32% of its value since the beginning of July. Micron has been affected by concerns about rising competition from China and a pullback in artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure spending due to higher interest rates, which could constrain funding. That's why Micron investors will now be eagerly awaiting Micron's fiscal 2027 first-quarter report, which is expected in the second half of December.

I will take a closer look at the state of the memory market to determine whether this high-flying AI stock can regain its mojo.

Market research firm Counterpoint Research notes that the memory supercycle continued in the third quarter of 2026. The dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) market's revenue increased by an incredible 5x over the prior-year period to $200 billion. A massive price increase drove this phenomenal jump, while bit shipments increased by only 4% year over year.

Counterpoint points out that Micron controlled 21% of the DRAM market last quarter. So, Micron's DRAM revenue in calendar Q3 should be $42 billion, based on Counterpoint's analysis. It is worth noting that Micron's fiscal Q1 ends in early December, so calendar Q3 overlaps with two months of its fiscal Q1.

But even if that's the case, Micron's DRAM business is on track to surge by a significant margin over the $10.8 billion in revenue it generated from this segment in the first quarter of fiscal 2026. Micron has called for $61.5 billion in revenue for the current quarter, implying a 4.5x increase over last year. The company derives nearly three-fourths of its revenue from the DRAM segment, suggesting it is on track to exceed $40 billion in DRAM revenue.

Also, the company is forecasting an increase of almost 8x in earnings per share from the year-ago period. Memory prices are still growing exponentially, as the limited supply of these chips forces buyers to compete for available capacity, thereby putting Micron in a position to command higher prices. As a result, the company should be able to achieve the phenomenal increase in earnings per share (EPS) that it expects.

However, a lot will depend on the company's guidance for the stock to accelerate again. There is good news for investors on this front as well.

Counterpoint Research expects memory prices to remain strong in 2027 as well. In fact, it isn't the only source that's expecting a bump in memory prices next year. TrendForce, for instance, is forecasting a 70%-140% increase in the price of AI-specific high-bandwidth memory (HBM) next year. The firm estimates that spending on memory and storage chips will account for 68% of the capital expenditure of the top nine cloud service providers (CSPs) globally, up from 47% this year.

Specifically, TrendForce expects a 50% increase in the capex of the top nine CSPs in 2027 to $1.38 trillion. That means memory and NAND flash storage revenue could hit $940 billion next year, up from an estimated $433 billion in 2026. As Micron is among the major manufacturers of DRAM and NAND flash, it seems well-placed to sustain healthy revenue and earnings growth next year.

In fact, as the memory market is poised to more than double in 2027, there is a chance of the company exceeding the 133% EPS increase to $176.50 that analysts are expecting for fiscal 2027. However, even that is a sizable jump, warranting a premium valuation for this semiconductor stock, which is trading at just 10 times earnings.

So, there are several signs suggesting that Micron stock could surge in December. The stock is extremely cheap, on track to deliver exponential growth, and could outpace Wall Street's expectations. All these factors could boost investor confidence in the stock and send it flying next month.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 790%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 202% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Data Quality Software Champions Recognized in Infotech's 2026 Emotional Footprint Report

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Drawing on verified end-user reviews from SoftwareReviews, Infotech's peer-review platform, the 2026 Data Quality Emotional Footprint Report recognizes Champions for customer experience and vendor relationships. The findings help organizations assess how providers support their customers alongside the capabilities needed to maintain accurate, consistent, and reliable data.

ARLINGTON, Va., Nov. 20, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Infotech has named four Champions in its 2026 Data Quality Emotional Footprint Report, recognizing four solutions that stand out for user satisfaction, trust, and overall customer experience based on verified feedback collected through the firm's SoftwareReviews platform.

Inaccurate, incomplete, and inconsistent data can undermine business decisions and create costly inefficiencies. Data quality solutions help organizations maintain accurate, complete, and consistent data across applications, databases, and cloud environments. Capabilities such as data profiling, cleansing, validation, standardization, duplicate detection, and ongoing quality monitoring also help IT and data teams identify and resolve errors, establish quality standards, and support governance requirements. These capabilities provide a more reliable data foundation for analytics, AI initiatives, and everyday business operations.

"Data quality is an ongoing business discipline, essential to trusted analytics, reliable AI outcomes, and confident decision-making," says Varun Palengetira, principal advisory director at Infotech. "Organizations should look for solutions that measure data reliability, connect data quality issues to business impact, and enable coordinated action across business and IT teams. It should enable teams to detect issues early, establish repeatable practices, prioritize remediation, and demonstrate measurable quality improvements. The greatest value comes from ensuring that the data supporting operations, analytics, automation, and AI is reliable enough to deliver the intended business outcomes."

Infotech's Emotional Footprint measures high-level user sentiment by aggregating responses across 25 proactive questions, creating a powerful indicator of overall user feeling toward the vendor and product. The result is the Net Emotional Footprint (NEF), a composite score that reflects the overall emotional tone of user feedback.

But Black Friday and Cyber Monday dates have expanded as retailers compete for customers. You can get the same Black Friday sales early, and we expect sales to wind down by December 2, 2026.

Black Friday used to be bigger for major retailers and more expensive tech and appliances, while Cyber Monday was better for cheaper tech and gave smaller businesses a chance to compete online. Nowadays, though, the distinction is almost meaningless. Every major retailer will offer sales on both days, and the smart move is to know what you want, do your research, or refer to guides like our Black Friday and Cyber Monday coverage that have done all the vetting for you.

Golden Globes Category Chaos: When ‘The Drama’ Is a Comedy…

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahead of the deliberations of the Globes’ eligibility committee, The Hollywood Reporter dissects a host of eyebrow-raising category submissions — ‘Club Kid’ as a drama, ‘Obsession’ as a comedy, and more.

The Drama is a comedy — or so say the backers of the film, about an engaged couple’s darkest secrets, in their submission for the Golden Globes, which requires all films be entered as either A) a drama or B) a musical or a comedy. And that’s not even one of this year’s more questionable submissions.

Other comedy entries include I Play Rocky, about the making of Rocky (which competed at the Globes as a drama); Obsession, about a young woman’s loss of agency; Tender Loving Care, about the frustrations of two young women, including the decline of one’s father; Artificial, about AI growing out of control; and Project Hail Mary, about a man sent into space against his will to try to stave off the end of the world. Meanwhile, Club Kid, about a man-child who ends up with a child — widely described as an elevated version of Adam Sandler’s comedy Big Daddy — has been entered as a drama. Related Stories Movies IDFA: 'The Year of the Ghost' and 'Memories of Lightning' Win Amsterdam Doc Fest Awards TV CBS Inks New Deal to Air Kennedy Center Honors

Yes, all of the aforementioned musical/comedy submissions have moments of laughter and all of the dramas have elements of drama — but still, these classifications are pretty eyebrow-raising, begging the questions: What is going on? And is there a better way?

The first Globes were held in 1944. There were no genre-specific Globes categories until the eighth, when the lead acting categories were split into two, one for dramas, the other for musicals/comedies. The following year, the same split was implemented for best picture. That category was reunified for the 11th ceremony, but the split was brought back for the 12th and every year since, helping to attract more stars to the ceremony.

Until 2023, Globes category submissions were made by studios and reviewed by the HFPA’s reminders list committee, which rarely overturned anything, even as dramas with music passed as musicals (Ray, Walk the Line and Rocketman), dramas with few laughs passed as comedies (Ghost, The Tourist and My Week With Marilyn) and comedies passed as dramas (All About Eve, Born Yesterday, Harvey and Sunset Boulevard — all in the same year). Once in a blue moon, the committee would reject a submission (Promising Young Woman as a musical/comedy), but it usually deferred to studios’ preferences.

Frustrations burst into the open in 2015 after The Martian, a film about an astronaut stranded on Mars, was submitted and accepted as a musical/comedy. Judd Apatow, whose comedy Trainwreck was in contention that year, tweeted, “Trying to dominate the comedy category when you are really a drama afraid of dramatic competition is a punk move.” The Martian ended up beating Trainwreck for best picture, something Apatow was still smarting over when he presented at this year’s Globes: “My film Trainwreck lost best comedy to Ridley Scott’s The Martian. You know Ridley Scott, America’s favorite comedy director — director of such hilarious comedies as Gladiator, Blade Runner and Alien? I can’t wait till later tonight when best comedy is won by Hamnet.”

But ownership of the Globes had changed in 2023 (the Globes now share the same owners as THR), resulting in widespread reforms. This year’s rules declare: “Motion pictures shall be entered in the category that best matches the overall intent, tone and content of the film. Therefore, a motion picture that is primarily intended to have a serious or dramatic tone but contains comedic overtones or elements shall be entered as a drama as opposed to a comedy. Likewise, a motion picture that is primarily intended to have a comedic or satirical tone but contains dramatic overtones or elements shall be entered as a comedy as opposed to a drama.”

Once the film submission period ends (Nov. 24 this year), an eligibility committee comprising current Globes voters (the quantity and names are kept private) evaluates every submission for “potential misclassification issues.” If found, they “may contact the studio … to discuss those issues and to resolve them informally.” Eventually, the eligibility committee meets with a review committee to discuss any outstanding concerns, and those committees — or at least members of them who have seen a film in question — vote to determine if a change is necessary; two-thirds must favor a change for one to occur.

Why do people try to game the Globes categories? Because the nationally telecast Globes ceremony provides the biggest spotlight for awards contenders before Oscar nominations voting (the 2027 Globes will take place Jan. 10, and Oscar nomination voting starts the next morning), making it highly valuable for a film or individual to be represented at the ceremony as a nominee or, better still, a winner.

There usually are fewer standout musicals and comedies than dramas, meaning it’s often easier to land a nom or win if a drama can pass as a musical/comedy. (This year’s aforementioned musical/comedy submissions surely wanted to get out of the way of The Odyssey.) Another motivation: Studios with multiple films prefer to not have them pitted against one another. (A24 probably wants Club Kid to compete as a drama because it already has The Invite and The Debut up for best musical/comedy, just as Neon probably wants Artificial to compete as a comedy because it already has All of a Sudden, Fjord and Paper Tiger up for best drama.)

It remains to be seen whether the Globes’ eligibility committee will overturn some of this year’s submissions. (Reports suggest it already rejected Michael as a musical/comedy.)

Moving forward, it would make everyone’s life easier if the Globes consolidated its genre-specific categories — picture, actor and actress — into single categories with 12 slots rather than divided categories with six each. Or, if the Globes prefers to continue to have multiple categories for each in order to yield twice as many winners, it might consider retaining the “drama” label for one but changing the other to “genre,” which would better describe recent musical/comedy nominees like Poor Things, The Substance and Bugonia.

StepSecurity raises $20M as hackers target the tools developers use

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Its researchers helped spot a hijacked Microsoft package the same week it announced the round.

StepSecurity’s graphic announcing its $20M Series A.

StepSecurity, a Seattle-area startup founded by two former Microsoft engineers, has raised a $20M Series A led by Elephant. It protects developers’ laptops, code repositories, package downloads and build pipelines from attackers who plant malicious code in open-source software.

StepSecurity, a Seattle-area security startup, has raised a $20M Series A led by Elephant, GeekWire reported. Runtime Ventures, Inner Loop Capital and SaaS Ventures also put in money, taking its total to $23M.

The company guards the places where software gets built, from developers’ laptops to the systems that test and ship code. Hackers now often hide bad code in free, open-source software. Once it runs, it can steal passwords and keys.

“A supply chain attack used to take a skilled team. Now one person can vibe code it,” CEO Varun Sharma wrote in a blog post.

The round landed in a busy week. On 17 November, hackers posted two poisoned versions of agent-framework, Microsoft’s Python toolkit for AI agents, which gets nearly 200,000 downloads a month. StepSecurity was among the firms that spotted the code, which stole passwords and keys, BleepingComputer reported.

Tools for coders have been behind other big hacks. Binance says its $6.3B hack started with a fake job interview and a tampered developer tool. Last month, a tampered Claude Code extension reached up to 5,000 Cursor users.

Sharma spent about 15 years at Microsoft. Co-founder Ashish Kurmi also worked there before leading security at Uber and Plaid. They started StepSecurity in 2021 after living through the SolarWinds and Codecov attacks.

More than 20,000 open-source projects use its free tool, Harden-Runner. Its researchers also helped track the ChainDrop npm worm in August. StepSecurity says its yearly revenue grew fivefold in both 2024 and 2025, but gave no figures.

Next, it plans to guard the sandboxes where AI agents run code. Worms such as Miasma already target AI coding tools. Rivals are growing too: Belgium’s Aikido became a $1B unicorn in January.

I write about startups, venture capital, digital policy, and Europe ecosystem, with an eye on the larger story beneath them: who gets to build the future, who profits from it, and how Europe is learning to speak in a louder voice of its own. Before moving into senior editorial leadership, I've built my career for over +10 years across journalism, storytelling, content strategy, SEO, and digital publishing, with experience in SaaS, hospitality, art, and culture.

James Webb telescope spots giant star swallowing its companion in first-ever observations of a stellar merger

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Astronomers using the James Webb Space Telescope followed up on years-old observations to confirm the first recorded act of a giant star swallowing its companion. The stellar cannibalism occurred just next door, in the Andromeda galaxy.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For the first time, astronomers have caught two stars — a supergiant and a star slightly smaller than the sun — in the act of merging. After orbiting each other for thousands of years, the pair came together in just under seven years — a span short enough that researchers could watch the drama play out from start to finish. The discovery was described in a study published Nov. 18 in the journal Nature.

"Stellar mergers are very, very difficult to observe in real time because they are hard to predict," Viraj Karambelkar, an astrophysicist at Columbia University and first author of the study, said in a NASA statement. "However, we noticed something unusual was going on in 2023 and continued to monitor the system to see what it would do."

The stars' imminent merger was originally picked up by NASA's now-retired Near-Earth Object Wide-Field Infrared Survey Explorer — a spacecraft that was designed to spot asteroids but also discovered many other interesting changes in the night sky.

Researchers noticed that a previously stable binary star system in the Andromeda galaxy, the closest major galaxy to ours, had begun to brighten. Follow-up observations from the Wide-Field Infrared Transient Explorer ‪—‬ a near-infrared instrument at the Palomar Observatory in California — revealed that the galaxy had also started spewing dust. Karambelkar’s team backed up these observations with data from the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) in December 2025.

The researchers dubbed the binary pair "Bao" (after its formal name, AT 2025abao) and started keeping tabs. They found that sometime in 2018, the smaller component of Bao bumped into the surface of the supergiant Bao. From there, things escalated quickly. The stars started exchanging gas in a process known as common envelope evolution. The larger Bao dragged its smaller companion closer and closer, until it was finally sucked into the supergiant's core, releasing a gigantic burst of energy.

Views of the stellar merger in Andromeda (left) captured by the NEOWISE, SPHEREx, and James Webb space telescopes (right).

This dramatic culmination was "almost like a bomb exploding inside a living star," study co-author Kishalay De, an astronomer at Columbia University, said in the statement.

The researchers were surprised by not only the speed of this interaction but also its outcome. Bao could have easily exploded into a supernova, transformed into a neutron star or collapsed into a black hole. Instead, it seems to have settled back into a single massive star. Future observations will give scientists a sense of how the new, rejuvenated Bao is holding up after its act of cosmic cannibalism.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

The team's analysis could also help inform future studies of supergiant stars and binary systems. Getting a clearer picture of how common envelope evolution works could allow astronomers to reconstruct how previous mergers went down. Thanks to NASA's new Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope, Karambelkar predicts Bao will be far from the last merging stars scientists spot.

Joanna Thompson is a science journalist and runner based in New York. She holds a B.S. in Zoology and a B.A. in Creative Writing from North Carolina State University, as well as a Master's in Science Journalism from NYU's Science, Health and Environmental Reporting Program. Find more of her work in Scientific American, The Daily Beast, Atlas Obscura or Audubon Magazine.

The 10 Coolest Cars We Saw at the 2026 Los Angeles Auto Show

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From Tesla’s thruster-equipped supercar to a modded Bugatti EB110 to a new Hyundai Elantra, La La Land’s latest car show has it all.

If you’re in downtown Los Angeles now through November 29 and want to get an up-close-and-personal look at some cool cars, head over to the convention center to see what’s poppin’ at the annual auto show. However, if you don’t have time to make the trip—or you’re nowhere near L.A.—and want to see the best cars that this year’s event had to offer, we've got you covered. While the 2026 L.A. auto show was short on major debuts or futuristic concepts, it hosted several headline-catching cars as well as multiple highly anticipated new models.

It’s one thing to see the Tesla Roadster through your smartphone screen; it’s another thing to see it about six feet away. While we’re highly skeptical that Tesla will ever publicly sell the thruster-equipped version that flew through the air and did a barrel roll, it’s more likely that a similar-looking, street-legal version will eventually reach production. Haters gonna hate, but the Roadster looks pretty damn cool, like Lamborghini designer Fabrizio Giugiaro was hired to style his version of the Lotus Esprit S1 that James Bond drove in The Spy Who Loved Me. Consider us shaken, not stirred.

Being in the same room as a Bugatti EB110 is about as common as being in the same room as Sir Anthony Hopkins. Like meeting the actor who famously played Hannibal Lecter, meeting one of the most iconic supercars ever made is unnerving. Now, you may have noticed that this example isn’t exactly all original; instead, it has been modified by Phiaro Inc., and it features a custom widebody kit as well as a chameleon-like paint job. The best part? Its modifications are completely reversible. Let’s just say it drew a crowd.

Genesis is going after the throats of luxury stalwarts like BMW, Mercedes-Benz and—dare we say—Bentley with the new GV90. The one you see here is the top-of-the-line Neolun model, and its most distinctive feature is these gigantic coach doors. Inside are four VIP-grade seats that treat passengers to an opulent cabin awash in rich materials and front seats that swivel 180 degrees to create a mobile lounge (this configuration is only accessible when the 657-hp EV is parked). The GV90 Neolun also has a veneer wood floor and 24-inch wheels that remind us of the iconic monoblock wheels made famous by Mercedes-AMG and Brabus. Few SUVs turn our heads, but Genesis appears to have another game-changer on its metaphorical hands.

We spotted this beautiful wagon-like Toyota Crown Signia from across the convention center’s south hall. Among a sea of generic crossovers, this low-slung longroof is an ode to an age when most Americans didn’t lust after the elevated seating position of a semi-truck. Of course, if you haven’t noticed, this isn’t exactly a production-spec Crown Signia. If you read our recent SEMA coverage, you may recognize it as the GT-S concept. Along with being lowered by five inches and wearing flared fenders that resemble the Mustang GTD, it has custom one-off coil-over dampers, 21-inch HRE wheels, and brakes from a Lexus RX500h F Sport. To all that, we say, “Hell yeah, brother!”

Let’s take a break from the world of flying cars, Bugattis, and fanciful electric SUVs to enjoy something that most people can afford: the new eighth-generation Hyundai Elantra. If its disorienting front lighting, heavily creased bodywork, and broad haunches intimidate you, we’re sure there’s a safer-looking alternative in Toyota’s showroom. But whatever you may think about the new Elantra’s styling, it definitely attracts attention. And Hyundai is hoping it’ll do just that to new customers who are looking to join the brand’s family tree. The nonhybrid version seen here draws power from a humble 147-hp four-cylinder engine, but the lineup will add a more powerful hybrid model sometime next year. While pricing is still TBD, we expect that it’ll cost around $25,000 to own the most interesting car in your cul-de-sac.

If you’ve ever watched an episode of Overhaulin’, a TV show that lasted for 10 seasons on TLC, then you’ve heard of custom car designer Chip Foose. Now, he has teamed up with Fox Factory Vehicles to create this even more muscular version of the new Ram 1500 Rumble Bee street truck. Along with unique wheels and body graphics, the interior has been restyled with diamond-stitched seats and Foose branding. The truck itself has a lowered ride height with an aftermarket coil-over suspension, and its supercharged V-8 is available with either 705 or 1000 horsepower. That’s a lot of horsies, and they’ll cost customers a lot of cash, with the Rumble Bee Foose Edition starting at $154,990.

This was one of the few concept cars at the show, but the Acura Nexera was first revealed back in August during Monterey Car Week. Still, the futuristic concept is really cool to see in person, especially with its dramatic glass canopy. Plus, how many times have you seen an Acura with butterfly doors? While the Nexera doesn’t preview an upcoming production model, it does provide a glimpse of Acura’s next-generation design language. Will the new 2028 RDX feature an active rear spoiler, carbon-fiber accents, and lighting that glows through the bodywork? Probably not. However, we can expect to see some of the Nexera concept’s interesting styling influence the brand’s next-gen compact SUV.

If you’re old enough to remember Acura’s legendary DC2 Integra Type R, you’ll definitely appreciate this new Integra Type S Heritage Edition. Not only does its Blazing Phoenix Yellow call more attention to itself than Big Bird starring in an old-timey picture show, but it also honors the OG Type R, which also wore a shade of Phoenix Yellow. The modern-day throwback sport compact packs the same 320-hp turbo four and six-speed manual as other models, but this one alone has copper-colored wheels and a titanium shift knob from the Honda Civic Type R. To get one in your driveway, you’ll need to be lucky enough to score one of the 700 limited copies for the U.S. market, but the asking price is a reasonable $58,995.

Have you been waiting for Dodge to stuff a Hemi V-8 in the new Charger? Well, you’re going to have to keep waiting for a while. However, if you’ve been hoping that the Brotherhood of Muscle will make a gas-powered Charger that’s set up for quick lap times, the new Super Bee variant should help you scratch that itch. Along with exclusive aero bits, stronger brakes, and wider summer tires, this track-focused Charger features an enhanced high-output Hurricane twin-turbo 3.0-liter straight-six, which is good for 600 horsepower (50 more than normal). The reimagined “Super Bee” logo on the rear quarter panel is one way to separate it from its siblings, but it also has an entirely different front clip that features new headlights and more open space to inhale engine-cooling air. If you’re wondering how much it costs, so are we, so stay tuned.

Like many of you, this was our first time seeing the new Chevy Silverado 1500’s new off-road-focused ZR2 variant. It’s a big truck, so big that we initially thought it was the HD version. Among the many changes to the newest generation of Chevy’s bestselling full-size pickup truck are all-new small-block V-8s. The ZR2 seen here is powered by a 481-hp 6.6-liter that helps churn massive all-terrain tires, which now stand 35 inches tall for the first time on a half-ton Silverado. The Ford F-150 Raptor-rivaling ZR2’s other party tricks include trick Multimatic DSSV spool-valve dampers. Inside, it includes GM’s first use of forged carbon fiber (neat!). If you want the most badass version, you’ll want to opt for the Bison Edition that adds beadlock-capable wheels, unique bumpers, and even more underbody armor. The cost of admission for the regular ZR2 is just under $80K, while the Bison starts even higher.

Eric Stafford’s automobile addiction began before he could walk, and it has fueled his passion to write news, reviews, and more for Car and Driver since 2016. His aspiration growing up was to become a millionaire with a Jay Leno–like car collection. Apparently, getting rich is harder than social-media influencers make it seem, so he avoided financial success entirely to become an automotive journalist and drive new cars for a living. After earning a journalism degree at Central Michigan University and working at a daily newspaper, the years of basically burning money on failed project cars and lemon-flavored jalopies finally paid off when Car and Driver hired him. His garage currently includes a 2010 Acura RDX, a manual '97 Chevy Camaro Z/28, and a '90 Honda CRX Si.

After Weil Gotshal’s collapse, law firm merger interest heats up

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Consultants Kent Zimmermann of the Zeughauser Group and Jeff Grossman of Oxford Strategic Legal Advisors both said they have seen merger interest increase, with Grossman noting that new candidates emerged after Weil, Gotshal & Manges’ collapse. (Photo from Shutterstock)

The fall of Weil, Gotshal & Manges is pushing law firm leaders to rethink strategy, and many are looking to find merger partners.

According to a Thursday story by Law.com, consultants Kent Zimmermann of the Zeughauser Group and Jeff Grossman of Oxford Strategic Legal Advisors both said they have seen merger interest increase, with Grossman noting that new candidates emerged after Weil Gotshal’s collapse.

“Many conversations were put on hold earlier this year to watch where the economy was going, how AI was going to effect the transformation of law firms,” Grossman told Law.com, adding that interest in mergers “was picking up over the summer, but now the pace is getting faster.”

“A large and growing group of firms want considerably larger balance sheets, greater long-term stability, more money to invest in talent and technology, and other advantages that come with scale,” Zimmermann added.

Lisa Smith of Fairfax Associates offered a slightly different read, telling Law.com that merger demand was already strong in 2026, and Weil Gotshal mainly added urgency for firms that were already considering deals.

“The lesson of Weil is that for firms that are looking to grow and looking to grow via merger or particularly for those starting to see some cracks in the firm, it’s important to go to the merger market while you’re still in a strong position,” Smith said to Law.com.

Smith suggested that firms should approach the merger market from a position of strength. Firms that wait until trouble sets in risk becoming what advisers call a “falling knife,” an unstable partner few want to take on.

“We’ve seen a number of occasions over the last few years where a firm needed to merge with a ‘white knight’ and can only get that when the firm is strong enough to be a desirable acquisition,” Smith said. “For firms that start to see signs that the future is going to be more challenging, it’s important to look at their options early.”

Grand Theft Auto VI dropped this week, and its release exceeds any release we've seen on the 2026 movie schedule. It's fitting because the game is loaded with all sorts of celebrities, including one unexpected face from one of the best reality shows, Big Brother.

We don't have the Christmas spinoff Reindeer Games planned for the 2026 TV schedule, but fortunately, a Season 23 Houseguest will keep gamers company if they have GTA VI. He may not have been invited to The Cookout, but Christian Birkenberger found his way into the biggest game of the decade.

Christian Birkenberger revealed on his Instagram that he voices three different characters in Grand Theft Auto VI. For those playing the game, here are the moments you might hear Christian, according to his post about being part of the game:

I've only logged a couple of hours into the game since its release, but I think the second voice-acting bit comes in during Jason's first mission in the game. Not a bad gig for a former Big Brother player who didn't even make the jury, and I'm happy to see him land a job like that after appearing on the CBS series.

Christian also added that his likeness was scanned, and that gamers could see him in a couple of the loading screens for the game. He wasn't sure that was happening as he hadn't seen the images yet, but I'll be keeping an eye out all the same.

Christian might be best known on Big Brother for his showmance with Alyssa Lopez, which quickly fizzled after his eviction. The nail in the coffin seemed to come when the Houseguests got videos from family members back home, and Lopez was shocked when she got a video from Christian rather than from her family.

Big Brother Is Reportedly Doing Another All-Star Season, And I Have One Hot Take About Casting

The couple announced they were dating on Big Brother's finale night, but less than 48 hours later, they broke up. He has not done any work in reality television since then, but Alyssa did two seasons of The Challenge after her exit.

As someone used to seeing the most memorable Big Brother players popping up all over the reality television circuit as of late, it's nice to see someone not in that circle doing something awesome. Amidst the narrative that doing the show doesn't come with any substantial amount of fame, we have a former Houseguest forever immortalized in one of the biggest video games of all time!

Grand Theft Auto VI is available to purchase right now, and Big Brother Season 23 is available to stream over on Paramount+. I'll be on the lookout for Christian's characters as I play, and eager to see what he has to say when I find him.

Teenage Engineering’s first CD player puts the spinning disc on display

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The EP/BC–1 can wirelessly stream audio to Bluetooth headphones or multiple devices using Auracast.

The EP/BC–1 can wirelessly stream audio to Bluetooth headphones or multiple devices using Auracast.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Teenage Engineering announced what it describes as “a modern take on a classic CD player” with its new EP/BC–1 featuring a slab-like design that leaves the spinning disc exposed instead of hiding it under a lid or inside a slide-out tray. The company says the EP/BC–1 is the first entry in its new broadcast series of products and was designed to bring “physical audio formats into a modern context, evolved from a shared obligation within the company to preserve the formats they grew up with.”

The EP/BC-1 is available now through Teenage Engineering’s online store for $299. The CD player joins the company’s existing EP–series of audio devices and has the same dimensions as its KO II synthesizer that launched in 2023 so it can be paired with, and connected to, other Teenage Engineering gear including the recently-announced EP-35 battery-powered amp.

Although it’s designed to play a nearly 45 year old medium, the EP/BC-1 features modern conveniences like variable speed playback, cue markers, a jog wheel for forward and reverse search, and built-in beat detection. There’s a very retro-looking series of digital displays showing track, index, time information, and audio levels, and around the edges you’ll find both digital and analog audio connections including a headphone jack and a USB 2.0 port.

If you want to go completely wireless the CD player has a rechargeable battery and supports both Bluetooth connections to headphones and Auracast streaming to multiple devices that support it. The EP/BC-1 is compatible with standard CDs as well as discs full of MP3 files, and when using the former it can rip tracks to 44.1 Hz/16-bit WAV files. If you want MP3s or other formats, you’ll need to convert them yourself.

Your Next Customer Is in the Chat. Why Last-Click Can’t Tell the Whole Story.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Snap’s Head of Performance Marketing GTM Rick Lucas and Kitsch CMO Ying Ying Kuang on why relying on last-click attribution hides your best customers, and how Clicks + Deterministic Views (CDV) paint a fuller picture of conversion.

Consumer behavior is changing, and marketers hunting for incremental growth need to look beyond traditional discovery.

As audiences turn to DMs and group chats to make purchase decisions, standard last-click platform reports miss authentic peer recommendations.

In this episode of Inside the Stack, Snap’s Head of Performance Marketing GTM, Rick Lucas, and Kitsch CMO Ying Ying Kuang explain why last-click attribution—while still essential—isn’t the whole story.

They discuss how Kitsch used a 16% geo-lift holdout test to prove Snap’s incremental value beyond the click, why Clicks + Deterministic Views (CDV) are necessary to capture real demand, and how to get comfortable living in the “messy middle” of continuous testing.

Philips Sonicare, a maker of electric toothbrushes and water flossers, decided to consolidate its streaming TV ad buys within Amazon, which has also been doing some consolidating itself.

After selling ads at The Wall Street Journal and WebMD and rising to CRO at The Trade Desk, Jed Dederick is now the first-ever chief revenue officer of creator startup ShopMy.

DecryptAds’ goal, Garcia said, is to give researchers, ad agencies, tech companies and regulators the evidence they need to fix security gaps in the programmatic supply chain.

The mobile growth marketing platform Liftoff reported its second-ever quarterly earnings last week. Shares were up by almost 25% after the company exceeded its revenue target and projected an optimistic profit margin outlook moving forward.

CTV sellers are promising measurable outcomes – even guaranteed store visits – but winning advertiser trust takes more than just measurement.

For the first time, a new feature in Google Ads breaks down the dollar amount a campaign saved from IVT blocking and exactly how much Google is crediting the campaign for bots caught post-billing.

10 Shows Like 'The Body' You Should Watch Next

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ross Johnson writes about television, film, and literature for Lifehacker. He has a degree in political science from the University of Rochester and has previously been a legal writer and editor for Thomson Reuters, for which he later traveled around India and the Middle East as an educator specializing in American English style and grammar for adults.

Ross has been an actor on stage, in commercials, and in independent films since childhood, and has written hundreds of articles and essays on genre literature and cinema for Barnes & Noble, Gizmodo, Kotaku, and Yahoo! Entertainment.Ross produces the Swan Songs film podcast and hosts The Sound of Tomorrow, a pop culture and current affairs radio show based in Rochester, New York, where he lives with his husband and dogs and supports the local arts and cinema scene. You can find him on Bluesky.

This is Streamalikes, where you can find a curated list of recommendations to stream based on what you already like.

Netflix's newest streaming hit, The Body, is drawing in millions of viewers, great reviews, and a gaggle of protests from people who didn't much care for the trailer. But if your lesbian-forward supernatural horror series isn't drawing cries of "blasphemy!"...what are you even doing? In that spirit, we'll conjure up some other shows dealing with the (extremely) dark side of high school.

This time-hopping survival drama is about a group of teenage girls who are stranded in the wilderness in 1996 and do terrible things to survive—the extent of which we only learn about via flashbacks from the present day, where the events of 19 months spent in the wild continue to resonate. There are ambiguous teases of the supernatural here (much more ambiguous than in The Body), but there's plenty of human-level horror as well. The show explores the nasty things we get up to when in survival mode (whether trapped in the wilds, stuck with other teenagers—or god forbid both at once), while also cynically (but accurately) positing that there's a huge difference between the version of the past we talk about and the one that really happened. Stream Yellowjackets on Paramount+.

Situated between flashbacks and flash-forwards (not entirely unlike Yellowjackets), mystery/thriller The Wilds sees an airplane full of teenage girls crash on the way to an empowerment program in Hawaii. It quickly becomes clear that the accident was engineered and that the whole thing is some sort of social experiment, and the survivors are forced to compete against each other if they want to live. The show understands the ways in which young women are exploited and expected to compete against each other in the real world, and the ways in which we'll do just about anything when survival is on the line. Stream The Wilds on Prime Video.

When pretty, popular girl Kate Wallis (Olivia Holt) goes missing without a trace in 1993, awkward Jeanette Turner (Chiara Aurelia) steps in to fill the gap, modeling herself after Kate by working her way into the missing girl's social circle and getting close with her boyfriend. That's all going great until Kate reappears two years later, accusing Jeanette of having witnessed the events around her disappearance and keeping quiet. Whoops. There's even more going on, as the show shifts back and forth between 1993, 1994, and 1995 to slowly reveal the missing pieces. Season two takes on a different story set in 1999 and 2000, and the forthcoming (surprise) third season will shake things up again. The emphasis here is on mystery, but it's a show about the aftereffects of trauma and of the dark secrets that can tear families and communities apart. Stream Cruel Summer on Hulu.

Juicy import Elite follows a group of working-class friends who earn scholarships to Las Encinas, a fictional private school that is, in the show’s universe, the most exclusive in Spain. What they find there is snobbery, mystery, murder, and lots and lots of sex (between and among characters of various sexual orientations and numerical groupings). The smart, wonderfully trashy show ran for a shocking (in our modern streaming era) eight seasons, with a couple of regional remakes. There's no supernatural stuff here, but plenty of dark deeds done with youthful vigor. Stream Elite on Netflix.

A teenager, Mae (Madeleine Arthur), is discovered by the side of the road with weird religious symbols carved into her skin. The hospital psychiatrist who treats her (Emily Deschanel) takes pity on, and interest in, Mae, deciding to take her in while arrangements can be made for a more permanent home for the cult escapee. Mae is, after all, level-headed and quiet, and couldn't possibly be any trouble. Hoo boy, yeah, that turns out to be a bad call on the good doctor's part. Stream Devil in Ohio on Netflix.

A different type of blasphemy at play here, in that Joan Lindsay's novel was already adapted brilliantly by Peter Weir in 1975, in a film often referred to as the greatest to come out of Australia. And yet, this Natalie Dormer-lead take is an achievement in its own right, the extended running time of a miniseries offering up more time to sit with the story's deliberately ineffable mysteries. Dormer plays Hester Appleyard, headmistress of a school for young ladies in the Australian bush. It's going well until a Valentine's Day picnic to the title's Hanging Rock sees three students and a governess disappear, impossibly, without a trace, bringing about the slow unravelling of pretty much everyone left behind. Stream Picnic at Hanging Rock on Prime Video and Tubi.

Mariam (Andria Tayeh) is brutally bullied by the cool clique at her private school in this Jordanian import, a high school drama that's both universal and distinctly Muslim in character. Fed up, Mariam convinces a couple of similarly outcast friends to join forces with her in a revenge plot that quickly gets out of hand. The show deals with Jordanian-style patriarchy head-on, without drawing any easy conclusions about its characters—Mariam catfishes one of her bullies and convinces her to send a revealing picture, one that she immediately posts, exploiting the very same repressive cultural elements that she claims to deplore. It's a smart, realist drama with soapy elements and nothing by way of the supernatural, but it's still a very sharp picture of the extremely cutthroat nature of high school life. Stream AlRawabi School for Girls on Netflix.

Peyton List stars as Maddie, a teenager in small-town Wisconsin for whom her death is very much just the beginning. Stuck in a high school in the afterlife (because hell is definitely for children), she goes on a journey to solve her own murder while uncovering secrets and lies in both worlds. It all sounds a bit grim, but the show has a sense of fun about it: While Maddie does develop the ability to communicate with the living, her world is populated by ghost teens from various eras and generations, with customs, mores, and cliques having developed in ghost high school just as in our world. Stream School Spirits on Paramount+.

The new Girigo app, claiming to grant wishes, sounds like harmless fun. Except, of course, that the things that teenagers want aren't always beneficent, and the app has already been the conduit for some incredibly dark deeds by the time it gets into the hands of the five high schoolers who find themselves caught in a Ring-like web of shamanistic horror and death countdowns. This is why we tell you to put your phones down once in a while... Stream If Wishes Could Kill on Netflix.

Four one-time best friends reunite at Rosewood High School a year after the disappearance of the leader of their little clique, all having received threatening messages from the mysterious "A," who knows everything they did last summer...and pretty much all the rest of the time. Shoplifting, parents having affairs, students hooking up with teachers—you name it, it's happening in town, and A knows it all. After the prime suspect turns up dead, the girls are caught in the killer's web while trying to keep their secrets hidden. If seven juicy seasons and three A's aren't enough for you, there are three spin-offs. Stream Pretty Little Liars on Hulu and HBO Max.

Ross Johnson writes about television, film, and literature for Lifehacker.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

York City boss Scott Lindsey has insisted midfielder Hiram Boateng is “in a good place” following his court appearance earlier this week.

At Manchester Magistrates’ Court, the 30-year-old pleaded not guilty to using threatening, abusive or insulting behaviour with intent to cause fear of, or provoke, unlawful violence.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

He is alleged to have punched a Rochdale fan during a pitch invasion that took place when the two sides met in the National League on April 25.

York have offered their support to Boateng, who has been training ahead of Walsall’s visit tomorrow (November 21).

“I haven’t had to [put an arm around Hiram],” said Lindsey, as reported by York Press. “I probably have subconsciously.

“I have spoken to him and he’s in a good place, he’s playing some really good football at the moment and is getting back up to speed playing some football after being out for a while.

“What was pleasing was when we were looking to bring him off around the 60 minute mark [against Rochdale], he was okay to stay on.

“He did get a little bit of a knock just before he came off, but he’s okay."

Boateng is charged with a section four public order offence that carries a maximum sentence of six months in prison.

A spokesperson for York said Boateng “maintains the full support of everyone at York City and is a much-valued member of the squad, both on and off the pitch”.

The ex-Crystal Palace man missed the early stages of the season through injury but has appeared in four of York’s last five League Two games.

“He’s playing some good football at the moment,” said Lindsey. “I think that his focus is on the football, which it needs to be.

“For all of the time that things are going on in the background, you’ve got to focus on something that’s good, and that’s good in his life at the moment.

Fourth-placed York could break into the automatic promotion places in League Two with a win over sixth-placed Walsall, who trail the Minstermen by just one point.

Silicon Valley’s reaction to the Nvidia drama is rife with blind spots

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hello again, and welcome back to Fast Company’s Plugged In.

Jensen Huang was scheduled to speak at this week’s Semafor Silicon Valley & The World conference, which he cochaired. For reasons too obvious to require explanation, he didn’t make it to the event. And yet he dominated the on- and off-stage conversation so resoundingly that it might as well have been titled “Silicon Valley, The World & Jensen Huang.”

As tech notables sat for interviews—among them Sam Altman, Clay Bavor, Jeff Bezos, Andy Jassy, Yann LeCun, Fei-Fei Li, and David Sacks—moderators kept requesting their take on Huang’s shocking ouster from Nvidia. Most lavished praise on his entrepreneurship, but begged off saying much more on the grounds that the circumstances remained murky. Bezos’s comments were typical: “I don’t know enough of the details and I think probably most of us don’t know. . . . What I can tell you for sure is that Jensen is a remarkable guy who founded and built a remarkable company.”

Speakers at Semafor’s event approached the subject more gingerly than many in the scrum on X where Huang declared on Tuesday that “if the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.” Skimming the 58,000 replies to his post, I didn’t see much discussion of the specifics of the case: Huang’s confessing to an extramarital affair with an Nvidia business partner who was allegedly also a spy for China, the questions that raised relating to Nvidia chips possibly being smuggled into China, and reports that he had two additional affairs with Nvidia employees.

Instead, Huang’s X followers mostly had his back. Tech industry celebs such as Aaron Levie, CEO of Box; Palmer Luckey, cofounder of Anduril; and Linda Yaccarino, former CEO of X, shared a few words (or emojis) of support. Others were civilians who expressed their enthusiasm for Huang’s theoretical rehiring in the form of truly weird AI slop: Jensen as Jesus at the Last Supper, Jensen popping out of a casket, Jensen lip-syncing Michael Jordan’s comments upon his return to the Chicago Bulls.

Despite reports of disagreement among Nvidia’s board members, the company continues to rule out Huang’s return. Given the unfolding legal and geopolitical elements of the story, a speedy Sam Altman-style reinstatement would appear to be fantasy in even the rosiest of scenarios.

The revelations thus far have already prompted the Trump administration to tighten restrictions on chip exports to China. Huang has agreed to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on December 1. Democrats, who will regain control of the Senate and House of Representatives in January, are salivating at the opportunity to dig into questions such as whether Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick facilitated the diversion of Nvidia chips to China. And California’s attorney general has subpoenaed Nvidia in search of any evidence that Huang’s affair led to chips or confidential information making their way to China.

Bottom line: Everyone who says we don’t know enough to come to a firm conclusion about the precise nature of what happened and its appropriate repercussions is on far firmer ground than those who think Huang was unfairly forced out and should be welcomed back.

But for all the miasma of uncertainty clouding this drama, it’s already told us plenty about Silicon Valley. Its most admired, envied, seemingly essential CEO being shown the door, even as the company he cofounded is still booming, is a deeply unsettling black swan event, and I haven’t seen many in the industry unblinkingly confront its implications. It’s surely not a coincidence that Gina Raimondo, the former U.S. commerce secretary—who is a technocrat, but not a creature of the Valley—had the bluntest assessment of the situation I heard at the Semafor conference: “Any kind of corruption, politics, dealing with spies, [there should be] no tolerance for it.”

Paul Graham, cofounder of Y Combinator, coined the term “founder mode” in 2024. It celebrates startup founders who refuse to cede control to professional managers, regardless of how big their brainchildren become. Decades before the concept had a name, Huang exemplified it. Instead of growing more remote from the minutiae of the company’s operations as it became a behemoth, he had his hands in everything—which is how he wound up with a reported 60 executives answering directly to him.

Over Huang’s 33 years as Nvidia’s CEO, he took risks that no MBA-brandishing suit would have dared. None was gutsier than his 2006 decision to pivot from focusing on 3D graphics to building a high-performance general-purpose computing platform. That prescience paid off when artificial intelligence became an epoch-shifting opportunity, leading to the company’s 90% market share in the chips that power generative AI.

In recent years, as Nvidia has jockeyed for the title of the world’s most valuable company, Huang has been no less daring. Whatever you think of the AI industry’s “circular financing,” in which many players take financial stakes in their own customers, Nvidia’s willingness to write huge checks to companies in its own ecosystem has been critical to AI’s growth. And as expectations for the technology prop up the stock market, it’s not just the tech business that might look very different without Huang’s high-stakes gambling. So might the entire U.S. economy.

Huang the public figure has become as unique as Huang the fearless entrepreneur. Once an unassuming engineer in an Nvidia shirt who went by his birth name of Jen-Hsun Huang, he reinvented himself into the genially bombastic, leather jacket-wearing rock star I interviewed in 2024. Only Altman rivaled his omnipresence as a speaker at tech industry events. Huang, who relished engaging in stunts such as frolicking with robots onstage, was a lot more fun. I can understand why people who never met him take his downfall personally.

If you can’t envision a tech industry without Huang at its core, you have every incentive to wish for any possible outcome that would reinstate him to his previous eminence. But Huang himself took risks that made a happy ending far less likely.

All evidence indicates that his initial strategy for dealing with the mess he created involved misleading Nvidia’s management and board, not to mention members of the press. Huang’s undisclosed relationships seem to have violated Nvidia’s own code of conduct. I have seen many people argue that they should still be forgiven. But to my knowledge, nobody has explicitly maintained that a CEO lying to his board is tolerable, at least if that CEO is Jensen Huang. Maybe that’s because the case is impossible to make.

Another bad bet Huang made was his tight relationship with President Trump, which he was flaunting as recently as September. It apparently influenced the president to dismiss an October 2025 intelligence briefing on Huang’s relationship with Megaspeed’s Alice (no relation) Huang. Trump may not have been doing Huang any favors, given that the story blew up in both of their faces at the worst conceivable time, after the Democrats’ wins in the midterm elections.

As Daring Fireball’s John Gruber noted this week, tech executives have historically steered clear of hugging any political party so close that it would complicate their relationships in Washington, D.C., should the balance of power shift. Huang abandoned this approach during Trump’s second term. So did other executives, but only Elon Musk may suffer graver consequences.

Huang’s unplanned departure also left Nvidia scrambling to deal with a fundamental bug in the logic of founder’s mode. Any company that’s lucky enough to matter for decades will, at some point, be run by a nonfounder—maybe even a dreaded professional manager. Responsible founders, no matter how hands on, should prepare for that day. Many do not.

Along with everyone else in the Valley, Huang apparently couldn’t picture a post-Jensen Nvidia. I hope he was kidding when he said he’d like to run the company for another 30 or 40 years and then be replaced by a robotic version of himself. But his failure to establish any clarity around a succession plan is a deadly serious problem for the company he founded. (His daughter, son, and son-in-law all work at the company, but not in roles that leave any of them as an heir apparent.)

Nobody else in tech ever had a career like Jensen Huang. His present quandary is part of that story, but I don’t expect it to be his only legacy. He might even rebound in some manner that doesn’t involve him getting his old gig back. Assuming the mystery surrounding this chapter eventually subsides, I hope the facts of the matter leave the industry wiser—if sadder—about aspects of its culture that it usually does its best to ignore.

You’ve been reading Plugged In, Fast Company’s weekly tech newsletter from me, global technology editor Harry McCracken. If a friend or colleague forwarded this edition to you—or if you’re reading it on fastcompany.com—you can check out previous issues and sign up to get it yourself every Friday morning. I’m also on Bluesky, Mastodon, and Threads, and you can follow Plugged In on Flipboard.

Siemens: Global Deal Enablement Architect

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Siemens Digital Industries Software is a leading provider of solutions for the design, simulation, and manufacture of products across many different industries. Formula 1 cars, skyscrapers, ships, space exploration vehicles, and many of the objects we see in our daily lives are being conceived and manufactured using our Product Lifecycle Management (PLM) software.

Siemens is a global technology powerhouse focusing on the areas of intelligent infrastructure, automation, and digitalization. Within Global Sales Excellence (GSE) HQ, our mission is to empower our sales and commercial teams with world-class operational frameworks, deal enablement structures, and streamlined processes. We champion operational excellence, ensuring that complex commercial strategies translate into clear, scalable, and frictionless execution for our teams and customers.

We are currently seeking a Deal Enablement Architect for the commercial and operational frameworks that enable products, business models and cross-business opportunities to be sold consistently, efficiently and at scale. The GSE HQ Deal Enablement Architect owns the design and evolution of the business-facing elements of the Global Deal Enablement Operating Model.

The role connects Product, Strategy, Pricing, Finance, Sales and operational stakeholders to translate commercial strategy into clear and practical guidance for the field. Its purpose is to ensure that Deal Guidelines, new products, new business models and cross-business propositions are designed for execution, with clear parameters, watch-outs and escalation pathways. The role challenges complexity at source and ensures lessons from execution are continuously fed back into better guidance and commercial design.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.

🇬🇧 United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland
